AgradecimientoS

En primer lugar, quiero expresar mi inmensa gratitud a mis directores, Iván
Martín y Fran García Tortosa, y al “jefazo” Pedro Alfaro. Desde antes de que todo
esto comenzase, me dieron la oportunidad de trabajar a su lado y me animaron,
pese a mis dudas iniciales, a emprender la larga y dura travesía por los caminos
de la investigación. Ya luego, durante los años de tesis, han estado siempre ahí,
apoyándome, orientándome y, en muchas ocasiones, aguantándome cuando
empezaba a verlo todo negro (el llorón). Gracias por todo el conocimiento que
me habéis aportado, los valiosos consejos que me habéis dado, y las vivencias
que hemos compartido. Gracias también por confiar en mi y por darme libertad
para llevar el timón y tomar mis propias decisiones. En definitiva, se podría
decir que más que directores, hebéis sido compañeros en este viaje.
En segundo lugar, quiero agradecer a todas aquellas personas que han colaborado
en los diferentes trabajos que estructuran esta tesis, sin los cuales no hubiera sido
posible sacarlos adelante: gracias a Leah Koch, de la Universidad de Stavanger, por
su incalculable ayuda en la interpretación y modelado de zonas de falla; al equipo
de Antonio Gil, de la Universidad de Jaén, por su gran labor en el aporte de
equipo y procesamiento de datos GPS; a José Antonio Peláez, de la Universidad
de Jaén, por iniciarme y asesorarme en el cálculo del potencial sísmico de fallas
activas; a Vicente Pérez Peña, de la Universidad de Granada, por su valioso aporte
en el cálculo de índices geomorfológicos; a Julia Castro, por esas inacabables,
pero memorables, jornadas de campo por la falla de Baza y en las trincheras; y a
Jan Tveranger y Jacob Dieset, por poner su granito de arena en las duras tareas
en las trincheras 3D.
Me gustaría hacer mención especial a Néstor Cardozo, de la Universidad de
Stavanger, y a Chris DuRoss, del Geologic Hazards Science Center del USGS.
Al primero, por por su gran colaboración en el desarrollo de esta tesis y por
acogerme con tanta confianza durante mi increíble estancia en Noruega. Al

A Enrique López Aparicio por su esfuerzo y su extraordinario trabajo de maquetación de esta tesis.
Al maravilloso personal del Hotel Galera, por tratarme como a uno más de la familia durante las
numerosas (y a veces duras) campañas de campo en Galera.
Pero sobre todo, quiero agradecer a aquellos que han estado en el backstage durante toda
esta trayectoria. Primero, a mis padres y a mi familia, por haberme dado las alas y el apoyo
incondicional que me han permitido llegar hasta donde estoy. Segundo, a Eva, por haber sido la
mejor compañera de vida durante estos años y haberme dado, en muchas ocasiones, la energía y
los ánimos para avanzar. Tercero, pero no menos importante, a mis amigos, a los de toda la vida,
pero también a Elara y Javi, que en los últimos tiempos siempre han estado ahí. A todos, me
alegro de que estéis conmigo aquí, al final de todas las cosas.
Por último, agradezco al Ministerio de Ciencia, Innovación y Universidades la concesión de la
ayuda predoctoral FPU (FPU16/00202) que, junto al proyecto de investigación Tectónica Activa
de la Subcuenca de Baza (TASCUB) (RTI2018-100737-B-I00), me han permitido financiar los
gastos de esta investigación.

<!-- p. 10 -->

Abstract

This PhD thesis is focused on the characterisation of the Baza Fault (BF) and the Galera Fault
(GF), the main active faults of the Baza Subbasin, located in the eastern sector of the GuadixBaza Basin (Central Betic Cordillera, S Spain). This research integrates different approaches,
including structural geology, tectonic geomorphology, palaeoseismology, tectonic geodesy, and
seismology. Moreover, we discuss the influence of these faults on the recent relief of the basin
during the exorheic stage (ca. 500-600 ka) and propose a local neotectonic model for the Baza
Subbasin, integrating both faults in the geodynamic setting of the Central Betic Cordillera.
Through geological mapping (1:5,000 scale) of fault traces and offset deposits, we define the
BF as a ca. 40 km-long, E-dipping fault that offsets Upper Miocene to Holocene deposits.
We divide the fault into two sectors according to its geometry: a narrow N-S to NNW-SSE
northern sector and a wide NW-SE southern sector. These geometric differences are the result of
i) basement differences between the north and south of the subbasin, ii) different orientations of
the basement BF with respect to the regional extension direction, and iii) interaction with other
active faults in their terminations.
We define the GF as a ca. 30 km-long fault, striking roughly SW-NE and dipping either
vertically or to the NW. This fault offsets Pliocene to Holocene deposits. The distribution of the
most recent endorheic deposits indicates tectonic control of the sedimentation. We divide the
GF into four sectors based on the geometry of the fault array. This fault array forms segmented
en échelon (sectors 2 and 4), anastomosing (sector 3), and linear (sector 1) patterns. These
patterns are caused by i) the orientation of the basement fault and ii) the variable thickness of
the sedimentary cover.
At the meso-scale, the BF and GF show highly complex and spectacular fault zone structures
that result from the deformation of poorly-consolidated, water-saturated sediments during the
endorheic stage of the Baza Subbasin. We characterise these structures in the BF, describing and
interpreting 13 successive sections in a 15x15x4 m trench (the floor section is also studied).
Then we integrate them to construct a 3D model of the fault zone. We conclude that the high
heterogeneity in the distribution and styles of deformation within this fault zone is controlled
by the throw and geometric variations of the fault strands and by the mechanical stratigraphy.
In future work, we will study analogous structures in the left-lateral GF, which also features an
exceptional fault zone and fault rocks.

<!-- p. 11 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

We analyse fault kinematics from meso-scale kinematic indicators and stratigraphic and
geomorphological markers and from the surface fault geometry. The BF presents pure normal
dip-slip kinematics in most of the fault zone. The GF is characterised by oblique kinematics,
with a main left-lateral component and a minor vertical component of displacement.
To quantify the fault displacement, we calculate long-term slip rates from offset markers (geologic
and geomorphic) and short-term slip rates from geodetic data of the Baza GPS network. Longterm slip rates for the BF range between 0.2 and 0.5 mm/yr, while short-term slip rates range
between 0.3 ± 0.3 mm/yr and 1.3 ± 0.4 mm/yr. The long term vertical slip rates for the GF range
between 0.02 and 0.05 mm/yr, while the short-term horizontal slip rate is 0.5 ± 0.3 mm/yr.
Geodetic data also show that the BF accommodates one-third of the WSW-ENE regional
extension of the Central Betic Cordillera. From these data we establish a tectonic model for the
Baza Subbasin, where the BF and GF are kinematically coherent and divide the subbasin into Resumen
two tectonic blocks, a SE block and a NW block. These blocks exhibit westward displacement,
but the relative velocities between them result in extension along the BF that is transferred to
the E along the GF.

Para cuantificar este movimiento, calculamos las tasas de desplazamiento de ambas fallas. Las tasas
La GF se extiende a lo largo del sector NE de la Subcuenca de Baza. Se trata de una falla de unos a largo plazo las estimamos a partir de marcadores estratigráficos y geomorfológicos desplazados
30 km de longitud, orientada SW-NE y que buza de forma pronunciada hacia el NW e incluso por ambas fracturas. Para la BF hemos obtenido valores de entre 0.2 y 0.7 mm/año (asumiendo

\1. Introduction

1.1. Interest of this study

The study of active faults consists of a detailed characterisation of these structures to analyse and
understand their behaviour within seismic regions and thus develop neotectonic deformation
models. This characterisation should be addressed in a multidisciplinary way, integrating
structural geology, tectonic geomorphology, geodesy and seismology.
The description and interpretation of the structure of fault systems is essential to better understand
and predict their behaviour. The analysis of the fault zone geometry, architecture, kinematics,
and evolution is important, for example, for understanding and predicting the impact of faults
on fluid flow in the upper crust, including groundwater flow (Bense and Van Balen, 2004;
Bense and Person, 2006; Folch and Mas-Pla, 2008), hydrocarbon migration, entrapment and
production (Grauls et al., 2002; Sorkhabi and Tsuji, 2005; Manzocchi et al., 2010; Wibberley
et al., 2017), hydrothermal flow and mineralization (Rowland and Sibson, 2004; Person et al.,
2008; Fairley, 2009), nuclear waste storage (Ofoegbu et al., 2001; Gray et al., 2005), and CO2
sequestration (Shipton et al., 2004; Agosta et al., 2008; Dockrill and Shipton, 2010).
Moreover, knowing the fault geometry and kinematics is one of the first steps in the seismological
and palaeoseismological characterisation of active faults (e.g. Cowie et al., 2012; Iezzi et al.,
2018; Mildon et al., 2016; Rockwell et al., 2009). Both the geometry and kinematics strongly
influence the seismogenic potential of a fault (e.g., Boncio et al., 2004; De Martini et al., 1998;
Pace et al., 2016; Wells and Coppersmith, 1994; Wesnousky, 1986), controlling the propagation
of fault ruptures during seismic events and hence the magnitude of the events. In turn, the
characterisation of the seismic potential of active faults is a fundamental step in probabilistic
seismic hazard assessment (PSHA) studies.
The Central Betic Cordillera is one of the most active tectonic zones on the Iberian Peninsula
(Sanz de Galdeano et al., 2012 and references therein) and is associated with the highest seismic
hazard, according to the Spanish seismic-resistant building code (IGN-UPM, 2013). On 31
September 1531, a severe earthquake destroyed several villages in the Baza region in Granada
Province (southern Spain). Historical documents state that this event was responsible for 400
deaths and severe damage in the towns of Baza and Benamaurel (Olivera Serrano, 1995).
Damage is also mentioned in remote towns, such as Orce and even Vélez Blanco (Martínez-

geological mapping of both structures, displaying the surface geometry of fault traces, offsetThese faults are considered the main active structures in the Baza Subbasin. The Baza Subbasin
materials, and associated structures. Corresponding maps at a 1:5,000 scale are attached toconsists of the eastern sector of the Guadix-Baza Basin (Fig. 1), a Neogene intramontane basin
this volume.located in the central sector of the Betic Cordillera. This basin has been studied extensively due
to its great interest among the scientific community:
• The Guadix-Baza Basin is located in a transition zone between two major geodynamic
sectors of the Betic Cordillera: i) the Eastern Betic Cordillera, which includes the Eastern
Shear Zone and is dominated by strike-slip tectonics (e.g., De Larouzière et al., 1988;
Silva et al., 1993), and ii) the Central Betic Cordillera, dominated by extensional tectonics
(Sanz de Galdeano et al., 2012; Galindo-Zaldívar et al., 2015). The Baza Subbasin is the
easternmost sector where active extension has been described within the Betic Cordillera.
• The sedimentary infill of the basin represents one of the most continuous successions
of continental Plio-Pleistocene sediments in Europe. This succession is the result of the
endorheic conditions of the Guadix-Baza Basin from the early Pliocene until the Middle
Pleistocene (e.g., Vera, 1970a and 1970b; Peña, 1979; Viseras, 1991; Guerra-Merchán,
1992; Gibert et al., 2007a and 2007b).
• The continuous continental Plio-Pleistocene sedimentary infill of the Guadix-Baza Basin
preserves a continuous palaeogeographic, palaeoclimatic and fossiliferous record, including
several palaeontological sites where a large number of mammal fossils have been found and
dated (e.g., Agustí, 1983; Martín-Suárez, 1988; Alberdi and Bonadonna, 1989; Guerra-
Merchán and Ruíz-Bustos, 1991; Maldonado-Garrido et al., 2017). This palaeontological
record includes the oldest hominid fossils in Europe (Toro-Moyano et al., 2013).
• The Guadix-Baza Basin presents a spectacular badland landscape that has become one of
the main attractions of the region and, together with the above exposed features, one of
the reasons why the basin was included in the UNESCO Global Geopark network as the
Granada Geopark (Fig. 1).
During Pliocene and Quaternary times, many of the characteristic aspects of the Baza Subbasin,
such as its geometry, structure, sedimentary environment, and landscape evolution, were strongly
influenced by the activity of the Baza and Galera Faults (Alfaro et al., 2008; García-Tortosa et
al., 2008b and 2011a). Several studies have focused on these faults. The Baza Fault was first
described by Alfaro et al. (2008). These authors provided a general description of the fault
array and estimated its maximum cumulative throw and long-term extensional rates. GarcíaTortosa et al. (2008b) made the first distinction of fault sections. These authors also described
the geomorphic expression of the fault, evaluating its activity through a geomorphic analysis of

According to the above factors, the main objectives of this PhD thesis are as follows: • With the results derived from the previous objectives, we establish two models: a model
of the role of active faults in the recent landscape evolution of the Guadix-Baza Basin and • To carry out a detailed structural characterisation of the active structures in the Baza
the Baza Subbasin, and a local tectonic model for the subbasin including the main active Subbasin, at different scales. The purpose of this characterisation is to obtain the necessary
structures and conforming with the current regional geodynamic setting of the Central data to properly define a detailed geometry, kinematics, and slip-rates of the Baza and
Betic Cordillera, which has further implications for seismic hazard analyses. Galera Faults. These data will provide information about the amount of deformation
accommodated by each structure and how it is distributed along them. The following
specific objectives are necessary for achieving this goal:
\* To study the sedimentary successions cropping out in the study area. This
study includes the definition of lithostratigraphic units offset by the active Baza
and Galera Faults. These units are dated by a detailed review of the available
chronostratigraphical data.
\* To obtain a structural characterisation of the Baza and Galera Faults based on both
surface and subsurface data, to define their geometry (length, dip, strike, width),
and to identify, describe and discuss their along-strike geometric variations.
\* To study, at a meso-scale, the structure of the fault zones. This characterisation
includes the description and interpretation of deformation and deformation styles
within these deformation bands and a discussion of the fault zone evolution.
\* To analyse the kinematics of the active faults, including a definition of the
direction and sense of slip through an analysis of meso-scale kinematic indicators
(e.g., slickenlines, joints and veins) and the fault surface geometry. This kinematic
analysis also includes a calculation of long-term and short-term fault slip rates using
stratigraphic or geomorphic markers, and geodetic data, respectively.
• To realize a seismogenic characterisation of the Baza and Galera Faults from structural,
palaeoseismological and seismological data. This objective includes two specific goals:
\* To provide a surface rupturing history and recurrence intervals; for this purpose, we
use the first field and palaeoseismological evidence available for the Baza and Galera
Faults. This evidence was obtained from several trenches dug on both faults.
\* To define the seismogenic potential and segmentation of the BF from geometric,
kinematic, and palaeoseismological data, defining its maximum expected magnitude,
mean recurrence interval and a geodetic rupture scenario.
• To understand and evaluate the tectonic control that these structures have exerted on the
landscape evolution of the Baza Subbasin and the Guadix-Baza Basin. For this purpose,
the following goals are necessary:
\* To evaluate, qualitatively and quantitatively, the effects of active faults on the
topography and the fluvial drainage network. This is achieved through an analysis
of the fault structure, high-resolution digital elevation models (DEMs), and the
application of several geomorphic indices.
\* To study the tectonic control of the active faults on the capture process in the
Guadix-Baza Basin by analysing the recent headward erosion process and valley
incision in the entire basin.

CHAPTER 2

GEOLOGICAL SETTING
AND PREVIOUS RESEARCH

<!-- p. 20 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

\2. Geological setting and previous research

The study area (Fig. 2) belongs to the Guadix-Baza Basin, a depressed region with an area
of 4,000 km2 located in the central part of the Betic Cordillera, in the northeastern region
of Granada Province (Andalucía, Spain) (Fig. 1). The Guadix-Baza Basin is divided into two
sectors: the western sector corresponds to the Hoya de Guadix (Guadix Subbasin), and the
eastern sector corresponds to the Hoya de Baza (Baza Subbasin).
The study area of this PhD thesis is located in the Baza Subbasin (Fig. 2), a region with an area of
approximately 1500 km2 and a mean elevation of 1,000 m above sea level. This area is adjacent
to other depressed regions, such as the Almanzora Corridor to the southeast and the Vélez Rubio
Corridor to the east (Fig. 1).
The margins of the Baza Subbasin are formed by the mountain ranges of the Betic Cordillera.
To the north, it is bounded by the mountains of Sierras de Cazorla, Castril (2,106 m), Seca
(2,136 m), Marmolance (1,518 m), and Montilla (1,804 m). To the east, the margin is formed
by the mountains of Sierras de Orce (1,822 m), Estancias (1,500 m), and Lúcar (1,772 m). To
the south, the subbasin is bounded by the mountains of Sierra de Baza (2.269 m). To the west,
the margin consists of a 25 km-long, N-S oriented mountain front (up to 140 m high) that
extends from the Sierra de Baza to Jabalcón Peak (1,494 m) and northwards (Fig. 2). This front
constitutes the natural border that divides the Guadix-Baza Basin into the Baza Subbasin and
the Guadix Subbasin.
The Baza Subbasin is intensely dissected by a drainage network composed of various main rivers
(Barbata, and Guardal Rivers) and their tributaries (e.g., the Castril, Baza, Cúllar, and Orce
Rivers), all of which belong to the Guadiana Menor Catchment (Fig. 3). All these streams flow
into the Guadiana Menor River, a tributary of the Guadalquivir River, which flows into the
Atlantic Ocean.

2.1. Geology and geodynamics of the Betic Cordillera

2.1.1. Geology of the Betic Cordillera

The study area is located in the central sector of the Betic Cordillera in southern Spain (Fig. 1).
The Betic Cordillera is a NE-SW oriented mountain range that belongs to the Alpine orogen
of the Western Mediterranean region and extends in the S and SE direction across the Iberian
Peninsula, from the Guadalquivir River to the Balearic Islands (Fig. 4). It is connected to the Rif
Cordillera (northern Morocco) through the Gibraltar Arc, forming a E-W elongated and arcuate
orogenic belt (approximately 600 km long and 400 km wide) that surrounds the Alboran Sea
Fig. 2. Map of the Baza Subbasin displaying the main towns, rivers, roads, and reliefs of the study area. Basin (e.g., Andrieux et al., 1971; Chalouan et al., 2001). This orogen is bounded to the north

Delga and Focault, 1968) or with the External Zones (Brouwer, 1926; Van Bemmelen, 1927;
Blumenthal and Fallot, 1935).
The External Zones (Fallot, 1948) (Fig. 4) comprise a SW-NE oriented northern band of the
Betic Cordillera, corresponding to the outer part of the Betic-Rif Arc. This domain is composed
of Mesozoic to Neogene, sedimentary rocks that were deposited on the Variscan basement of
the Iberian massif, along the passive continental palaeomargin of Iberia (García-Hernández et
al., 1980). The convergence of the Alboran plate and Iberia during the Miocene (Andrieux et
al., 1971) led to the deformation of this palaeomargin. The result was a belt of north-vergent
folds and thrusts that overthrust the Iberian Massif in a thin-skinned tectonic style (e.g., Banks
and Warburton, 1991; Nebbad, 2001; Platt et al., 2003; Meijninger and Vissers, 2007). The
External Zones are divided into two tectono-palaeogeographical zones (Blumenthal, 1927;
Fallot, 1948): the Prebetic Zone and the Subbetic Zone (Fig. 4). The Prebetic Zone comprises
successions deposited on the continental shelf of Iberia and features shallow marine and paralic
facies (mainly carbonates). In the Central Betic Cordillera, the Prebetic Zone is formed by the
Cazorla Arc, an arcuate imbricate of thrusts (e.g., Banks and Warburton, 1991; Nebbad, 2001;
Platt et al., 2003). The Subbetic Zone is interpreted as sedimentary successions deposited on the
deep marine basin adjacent to the Iberian continental shelf and is dominated by pelagic facies
(García-Hernández et al., 1980). This zone is deformed mainly by a series of north-vergent

2.1.2. Geodynamic and palaeogeographic evolution of the Betic
Cordillera

The geodynamic setting of the Betic Cordillera has been conditioned by the relative motion
between the Nubian and Eurasian plates since the Late Cretaceous. In the Late Cretaceous,
convergence between these two tectonic plates started, triggering the onset of the Alpine Orogeny
in the Western Mediterranean region. During the initial stage (Fig. 5A), this convergence caused
compression that led to crustal shortening and thickening (e.g., Dewey et al., 1989; Mazzoli and
Helman, 1994; Rosenbaum, et al., 2002). This compression affected the Mesomediterranean
plate, a tectonic domain that was located between the Nubian and Eurasian plates in the middle
of the present Mediterranean Sea during this period (e.g., Bouillin et al., 1986).
During the late Oligocene-early Miocene, widespread extension occurred in the western
Mediterranean region related to the opening of the Argelo-Provençal Basin and the successive
formation of oceanic lithosphere (Boillot et al., 1984) (Fig. 5B). This extension caused break-up
and disaggregation of the Mesomediterranean plate into several domains that were displaced as
a result of escape tectonics. One of these domains, known as the Alboran plate (Andrieux et al.,
1971), migrated westwards through the Mediterranean during the Miocene (e.g., Tapponnier,
1977; Durand-Delga and Fontboté, 1980; Sanz de Galdeano, 1983; Leblanc and Olivier, 1984;
Martín-Algarra, 1987) (Fig. 5C). The westward drift resulted in the oblique collision of the
Alboran plate with the Iberian and North-African palaeomargins, giving rise to the Betic-Rif
Orogen. The collision caused WNW-ESE compressive and dextral transcurrent tectonics in
the Mesozoic to Cenozoic rocks of the continental palaeomargin of Iberia, forming the Betic
External Zones (e.g., Sanz de Galdeano, 1990; Banks and Warburton 1991; Nebbad 2001; Platt
et al. 2003; Meijninger and Vissers 2007). The Alboran plate itself formed the Betic-Rif Internal
Zones (Fallot, 1948). It presented westward migration during the Miocene, which in the Betic
Cordillera may have been accommodated by a major dextral transcurrent structure (Fig. 5C)
known as the Cadiz-Alicante Fault (Sanz de Galdeano, 1983, 1990a, 1990b, and 2008).
This event also led to the development of an arc-shaped subduction zone involving the subduction
of thinned continental (both Iberian and African lithosphere) and Atlantic oceanic lithosphere Fig. 5. Sketches illustrating the palaeogeographic evolution of the Western Mediterranean region and the

and Spakman, 1993; Zeck, 1996; Gutscher et al., 2002; Pedrera et al., 2011; Galindo-Zaldívar et

thousand metres (Sanz de Galdeano and Alfaro, 2004). The highest rates of uplift of the Betic (Fig. 7) (Serpelloni et al., 2007; Pérez-Peña et al., 2010a; Vernant et al., 2010; Mancilla et al.,

de los Filabres and Cazorla antiforms (Sanz de Galdeano and Alfaro, 2004). However, recent accommodated mainly by NNW-SSE-striking normal faults, e.g., the Padul-Nigüelas, Baza,

(Galindo-Zaldívar et al., 2015). 1999; Alfaro et al., 2001; Marín-Lechado et al., 2005; Sanz de Galdeano et al., 2012 and 2020).
Uplift continued during the late Miocene, leading to a progressive emergence of the W-E
to WSW-ENE marine basins (Braga et al., 2003), closing the connection between the
Mediterranean Sea and the Atlantic Ocean (Fig. 8B). In the early Messinian (Martín et al. 2014),
the deformation produced by the NNW-SSE normal faults and E-W strike-slip fault systems
caused the transformation of the closed seaways into intramontane basins (Galindo-Zaldívar
et al., 2003), such as the Granada and Guadix-Baza basins (Rodríguez‐Fernández and Sanz de
Galdeano, 2006; Ruano et al., 2004; Alfaro et al., 2008; Haberland et al., 2017).
To explain the extension in the Central Betic Cordillera in an interplate convergence setting,
several hypotheses have been proposed. Some models suggest that the extension may be caused
by post-orogenic extensional collapse following convective removal of the root of the lithospheric
mantle (Doblas and Oyarzún, 1989; Platt and Vissers, 1989) or by detachment and sinking
of the subducting lithosphere slab under the Alboran plate (Zeck, 1996). However, the most
accepted hypotheses are the following:
• The present-day extension in the Central Betic Cordillera is related to a back-arc
extension. This back-arc extension is the result of rollback processes associated with an
active arc-shaped subducting slab of thinned continental and oceanic lithosphere beneath
the Gibraltar Arc (Fig. 9A) (e.g., Zeck, 1996; Gutscher et al., 2002; Duggen et al., 2005;
Pedrera et al., 2011; Ruiz-Constán et al., 2011; Galindo-Zaldívar et al., 2015; González-
Castillo et al., 2015). In this model, the extension mainly affects the thinned continental
crust in the Alboran Sea and the surrounding region, as well as the thick crust in the Betic
Cordillera at upper crustal levels (Galindo-Zaldívar et al., 2015). However, although
seismic tomographic data show evidence of an E-dipping subducted slab (Calvert et al.,
2000; Gutscher et al., 2002; Wortel and Spakman, 2000), some authors suggest that
this subduction zone may not have been active since the late Miocene. This is supported
by several facts (Pedrera et al., 2011): i) the Miocene normal faults in the Alboran Sea
Basin were reactivated as strike-slip and reverse faults during Pliocene-Quaternary times
(Bourgois et al., 1992; Woodside and Maldonado, 1992; Martínez-García et al., 2011);
ii) the accretionary wedge related to the subduction zone is sealed by undeformed late
Miocene-Pliocene sediments (Rosas et al., 2009; Zitellini et al., 2009); and iii) the

Cordillera, showing the regional uplift and the resulting closure of the seaways (modified from Braga et al.,
2003). The red star in A) corresponds to the location of the future Guadix-Baza Basin. The red dotted line in B) • The present-day extension is related to lithospheric delamination. This delamination
marks the boundary of the Guadix-Baza Basin. affects part of the subducted slab composed of thinned continental lithosphere beneath the
Central Betic Cordillera. The detached slab is inferred to still be connected to the W-NW
Iberian thinned crust but is interrupted by a major crustal and upper mantle discontinuity
along its eastern side, to the west of Sierra Nevada (Banda et al., 1993; Andeweg and

<!-- p. 26 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

Cloetingh, 2001; Mancilla et al., 2013 and 2015). Extensional processes may be triggered 2.2. The Guadix-Baza Basin and the Baza Subbasin
by the emplacement of asthenospheric material between the underthrusted slab and the
Alboran plate through this discontinuity (Fig. 9B). This hypothesis is supported by the
existence of intermediate deep earthquakes in the region that do not resemble subduction-
related stress field earthquakes. In addition, the GPS velocity field and source mechanisms 2.2.1. Geological setting and evolution
of shallow earthquakes show active extension above the delaminated slab (Central Betic
Cordillera) (e.g., Platt and Vissers, 1989; García-Dueñas et al., 1992; Docherty and Banda, The study area, the Baza Subbasin, is part of the Guadix-Baza Basin, the largest of the Neogene-
1995; Calvert et al., 2000; Fadil et al., 2006; Mancilla et al., 2013 and 2015). Quaternary intramontane basins (4,000 km2) in the Central Betic Cordillera (Vera, 1970a and
1970b) (Fig. 10). The sedimentary infill of the Guadix-Baza Basin overlies the contact between
the Internal Zones (south of the basin) and the External Zones (north of the basin) of the Betic
Cordillera. Many stratigraphic, sedimentological and palaeontological studies were developed in
the Guadix-Baza Basin. In the last decade, many works have focused on the active tectonics of
the Guadix-Baza Basin, describing active faults within the basin for the first time. These works
have helped to unravel the geological and palaeogeographical evolution of the basin.
During the Tortonian, the sector where the Guadix-Baza Basin would later develop was part
of a marine corridor known as the North Betic Strait (Colom, 1952) (Fig. 8A). The basin
was connected to the Atlantic Ocean through the Granada and Guadalquivir basins, and to
the Mediterranean Sea through the Almanzora and Campo Coy basins (Soria et al., 1999).
Sedimentation in the Guadix-Baza Basin began during this time, after the partial closure of the
North Betic Strait due to regional uplift in the orogen. Fully marine sedimentation persisted in
the Guadix-Baza Basin until at least 7.8 to 6 Ma (e.g., Soria et al., 1999; Hüsing et al., 2010).
During the late Tortonian-Messinian, continuous tectonic uplift resulted in the total closure
and establishment of the Guadix-Baza Basin as an individual basin dominated by continental
sedimentation (Fig. 8B) (e.g., Rodríguez-Fernández, 1982; Peña, 1985; Sanz de Galdeano and
Vera, 1992; Vera et al., 1994; Fernández et al., 1996; Soria et al., 1999; García-Aguilar and
Martín, 2000; Corbí et al., 2012).
Consequently, the sedimentary infill of the Guadix-Baza Basin consists of upper Miocene marine
deposits and Pliocene-Quaternary continental sediments (e.g., Vera, 1970a and 1970b; Peña,
1979, 1985; Viseras, 1991; Vera et al., 1994; Soria et al., 1987; Guerra-Merchán, 1992; Gibert
et al., 2007a and 2007b; García-Aguilar and Martín, 2000; García-Aguilar and Palmqvist,
2011). The Guadix-Baza Basin was a continental endorheic (i.e., internally drained) basin from
the early Pliocene until the Middle Pleistocene (Gibert et al., 2007a and 2007b; García-Tortosa
et al., 2008a and 2011a). It presents one of the most continuous successions of continental Plio-
Pleistocene sediments in Europe, preserving a continuous and exceptional palaeogeographic,
palaeoclimatic and fossiliferous record in the region.
The thick Plio-Pleistocene sedimentary succession was deposited due to continuous and relative
subsidence derived from ENE-WSW regional extension (Galindo-Zaldívar et al., 1993 and
2015; Braga et al., 2003; Sanz de Galdeano and Alfaro, 2004; Stich et al., 2006; Serpelloni et al.,
2007; Pérez-Peña et al., 2010a), which has controlled the geometry and structure of the Guadix-
Baza Basin since late Miocene times. The Guadix-Baza Basin and specifically the Baza Subbasin
present the lowest minimum Bouguer anomaly values in the entire Betic Cordillera (Mezcua et
al., 1996; Alfaro et al., 2008). The subsidence is accommodated by NNW-SSE-oriented normal

NE-SW oriented faults (Sanz de Galdeano and Peláez, 2007; Sanz de Galdeano et al., 2007a; fans on the borders that gradually connected by means of channel systems with a large eastern
Sanz de Galdeano, 2008). During the Plio-Pleistocene endorheic stage of the Guadix-Baza Basin lake (Peña, 1979; Viseras, 1991; Gibert et al., 2007a and 2007b). This lake represented the base
(Fig. 11A), the position of these depocentres controlled the distribution of the sedimentary level of the western fluvial system. During this endorheic stage, an extensive depositional-erosive
facies of the basin infill (Sanz de Galdeano et al., 2007a; Alfaro et al., 2008). glacis developed in the Guadix-Baza Basin. This glacis was a concave surface with a gentle slope
(between 2° and 6°) from the basin margins (average altitude of 1050 m) towards the centre
(García-Tortosa et al., 2008b).
Extensive sedimentation ended in the Middle Pleistocene (Díaz-Hernández and Juliá, 2006;
Scott and Gibert, 2009; García-Tortosa et al., 2011a). At this time, the Guadix-Baza Basin
became exorheic, as its drainage was captured by the Guadiana Menor River, a tributary of the
Guadalquivir River, and drained towards the Atlantic Ocean (Fig. 11B).
Some authors suggested that the capture process was tectonically controlled. Calvache and
Viseras (1997) proposed that the uplift in the Central Betic Cordillera caused a northward tilt
and step faulting in the basin that conditioned the zone (on the northern margin of the Guadix
Subbasin, Fig. 13) where the palaeo-fluvial network of the basin was captured by a tributary of
the Guadalquivir River. García-Tortosa et al. (2008a) established a relation between the capture
zone and diapiric and fault lineations along the Guadiana Menor River valley. Similarly, Moral
and Balanyá (2020) associated the capture zone with the SE termination of the Tíscar fault zone.
Hence, the subsequent Middle Pleistocene-Holocene history of the basin has been dominated
by erosional processes (Fig. 11C) (Calvache and Viseras, 1997; Díaz-Hernández and Juliá,
2006; Pérez-Peña et al., 2009), with sedimentation constrained to marginal alluvial fans, valley
bottoms, travertines, and fluvial terraces. Consequently, the glacis was progressively eroded, and
a complex fluvial drainage network developed across the Guadix-Baza Basin (García-Tortosa et
al., 2011a). The high mean elevation of the basin, the semiarid climate and the high erodibility
of the basin-fill sediments has led to intense incision by the drainage network and the formation
of a badland landscape (Fig. 14) that is still active (García-Tortosa et al., 2011a). The interval of
the main incision of the drainage network was estimated by Díaz-Hernández and Juliá (2006)
to have been between 115 and 48 ka.
Several ages have been proposed for the endorheic-exorheic transition of the basin. Most of the
previously reported ages are based on only two data points: the age of the youngest endorheic
sediments and that of the oldest exorheic deposits. Azañón et al. (2006) and Pérez-Peña et al.
(2009) suggested the youngest age for capture based on the age of endorheic deposits. They
propose an age of 42 ka based on radiometric dating of edaphic calcrete deposited above
the last endorheic lacustrine deposits. Several authors (Botella et al., 1983; Martín-Penela,Fig. 10. Geological map of the Guadix-Baza Basin including the main tectonic structures. BF: Baza Fault, GF:

2.2.2. Subsurface structure of the Baza Subbasin

Subsurface data are scarce in the Baza Subbasin. At a more regional scale, a seismic refraction/
wide-angle reflection survey carried out in the Central Betic Cordillera resulted in a ca. 275 km
long, NW-SE profile that crossed the Baza Subbasin. It allowed the authors to constrain the
crustal thickness in this sector of the cordillera to between 34 and 38 km (Banda et al., 1993).
The study area has also been crossed by two crustal deep-seismic profiles: ESCIBETICAS-1 and
ESCIBETICAS-2 (García-Dueñas et al., 1994; Jabaloy et al., 1995; Galindo-Zaldívar et al.,
1997). However, these profiles did not provide structural information on the Baza Subbasin.
This is because they show little reflectivity in their shallower part (0-2 s), as they were designed
for the study of the deep structure of the basin (Jabaloy et al., 1995).
At a local scale, the first subsurface data were obtained by the FAO (Food and Agriculture
Organization of the United Nations). In 1968 and 1969, the FAO carried out a series of vertical
electric soundings (VES) in the sector surrounding Jabalcón Peak to the E and W of the BF
trace. In these soundings, the authors identified an abrupt depression in the basement surface
and a significant increase in the Pliocene to Quaternary basin fill in the hanging wall of the fault.
These surveys were complimented with a 35 km-long, NW-SE reflection seismic profile (BT-2)
conducted by the ITGE (Instituto Tecnológico Geominero de España) in 1999 (available at http://
info.igme.es/infogeof/). This profile crosses the BF and situates the reflector corresponding to
the sharp contact between the Triassic basement and the sedimentary infill of the Baza Subbasin
at a depth of 2150 m in the fault hanging wall (Alfaro et al., 2008). This reflector shows an
increase in the sedimentary cover thickness towards the SE, as shown in the VES. However, the
obliquity of the orientation of this profile is not favourable for providing information about the
Baza fault zone or the deep structure of the Baza Subbasin.
Eight gravity profiles and a Bouguer anomaly isoline map were presented by Alfaro et al. (2008)
to estimate the depth of the basement in the hanging wall of the BF (Fig. 15A). Through these
profiles, they identify the maximum negative values of the Bouguer anomaly in two depocentres
in the hanging wall of the BF, close to its trace (ranging between -25 and -32 mGal), and a
gradual increase towards the E and NE. They relate these anomalies to the BF activity and
estimate a vertical cumulative throw of more than 2,000 m using the contact between the
basement and the sedimentary infill as a marker. In the gravity profiles, these authors also define
the BF terminations.
The most recent geophysical data from the study area come from three seismic reflection lines
(each 18 km long) acquired by Haberland et al. (2017). These profiles reveal the deep architecture
of the Baza Subbasin, showing a highly asymmetric structure, with the maximum observed
thickness of the sedimentary infill (2,200 m) close to the BF. They also reflect how individual
strands of the BF truncate the basin sediments (Fig. 15B).
In addition to the above geophysical information, subsurface data from the Baza Subbasin were
also obtained from a series of boreholes (81 to 310 m depth) drilled in the southern part of the
study area (Urbano et al. 1991). The basement-sedimentary cover contact in the subbasin was
penetrated in two of these boreholes located in the BF footwall, while it was not reached by those
drilled in the hanging wall, revealing the asymmetry produced by the BF subsidence.
Fig. 15. A) Bouguer anomaly isoline map (mGal) and gravity profiles presented by Alfaro et al. (2008) for the
Baza Subbasin. B) Seismic profiles of Haberland et al. (2017). We only represent the two profiles where the
BF is present.

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

A number of studies were carried out in the NE sector of the Baza Subbasin after the publication
of the faunistic catalogue of Moyà-Solà et al. (1981). Several palaeontological sites have been
dated along the Galera-Huéscar-Orce area (Fig. 16) (e.g., Agustí and Martínez-Suárez, 1984;
Mazo et al., 1985; Agustí, 1986; Agustí et al. 1987, 1997; Sesé, 1989; Ruíz-Bustos, 1991; Gibert
et al., 1994; Oms et al., 2000; Blain, 2003; Blain and Bailon, 2010). Martín-Suárez (1988)
described and studied the biostratigraphy of many of the sites of this area, such as Botardo (late
Miocene), Cañada del Castaño (early Pliocene), Galera (early Pliocene to Early Pleistocene),
Orce (Early Pleistocene) and Venta Micena (Early Pleistocene).
The Baza-Caniles area in the southern Baza Subbasin was studied by Guerra-Merchán and Ruíz-
Bustos (1991 and 1992); Guerra-Merchán et al. (1991) and Guerra-Merchán (1992), who dated
several sites of the lacustrine succession to the late Miocene to early Pliocene (Aljibe 2-3, Colorado
1-2, Cuzo 1-2-3, Santa and Cómodo sites) and Early (Areba site) to Middle Pleistocene (Caniles
site) (Fig. 16). In the Zújar area, Agustí and Oms (1998) and Agustí et al. (2001) dated the lower
part of the fluvio-alluvial sediments of the Guadix Formation to the early Pliocene (Zújar-4, 10
and 11 sites) and its upper part to the Early Pleistocene (Zújar-14 site) (Fig. 16).
In recent decades, palaeontological studies on the micromammal fauna in the Baza Subbasin have
been performed (e.g., Agustí and Madurell, 2003; Furió, 2003; Agustí et al., 2013 and 2015;
Martínez-Navarro et al., 2015; Piñero et al., 2017). The extensive catalogue of the vertebrate
fossil record made by Maldonado-Garrido et al. (2017), which includes most of the above cited
sites (Fig. 16), is particularly noteworthy.
Magnetostratigraphic surveys have also been conducted in the Baza Subbasin to recalibrate or
refine the chronologies of some of the stratigraphic sections, such as in the Cortes de Baza
(Oms et al., 1994; Agustí et al., 1997), Galera (Garcés et al., 1997), Zújar (Oms et al., 1999),
Cúllar-Baza (Gibert et al., 2007b) and Orce (Scott, et al., 2007; Oms et al., 2011) section. Some
authors have also dated some sites and made stratigraphic correlations between different areas
based on amino acid racemization data (e.g., Torres et al., 2003; Ortiz et al., 2000 and 2004).

2.2.5. Recent tectonics

In the Guadix-Baza Basin, ENE-WSW regional extension (2.1 and 3.7 mm/yr; Serpelloni et al,
2007; Stich et al., 2007; Pérez-Peña et al., 2010a; Galindo- Zaldívar et al., 2015) is accommodated
by NNW-SSE-striking normal faults sub-perpendicular to the extension (Galindo-Zaldívar et
al., 1989 and 1999; Sanz de Galdeano et al., 2012 and 2020) and SW-SE- to W-E-oriented
strike-slip faults parallel to extension. However, the recent tectonics of the basin have not been
extensively studied. The palaeostress history was studied from diapirism-related anticlines (López-
Garrido and Vera 1974) located to the west of Jabalcón Peak (Estévez et al., 1978). These authors
postulated that, apart from the regional extension, the Guadix-Baza Basin featured a compressive
stress field during Pliocene to Quaternary times. The work published by Sanz de Galdeano et al.
(2012) provides a compilation of the active faults in the Guadix-Baza Basin, but some of them
do not present evidence of recent activity (the Tíscar, Guadiana Menor and Alcóntar faults;
Fig. 16. Map of sedimentary facies (modified from Torres et al., 2003) including the dated palaeontological Foucault, 1971; Sanz de Galdeano et al., 2006b), are inferred only from geophysical data (the
sites of the Baza Subbasin (circles). Benamaurel and Cúllar faults; Sanz de Galdeano et al., 2007a), and/or are too short (i.e., less
than 10 km long, e.g., the Hijate, Negratín and Alicún faults).
In the Guadix Subbasin (western sector of the Guadix-Baza Basin), several normal faults are

2.2.6.1. Palaeoseismicity

In addition to historical and instrumental records, evidence of palaeoseismicity during Pliocene-
Quaternary times has been found in the Baza Subbasin.
In Alfaro et al. (1997), soft-sediment deformation structures are identified and described in
the Pliocene to Quaternary, marginal lacustrine sediments of the western and eastern sectors
of the Baza Subbasin (Baza and Cúllar areas) (Fig. 18). The authors interpret these structures
as seismites, i.e., formed by liquefaction and/or fluidization processes induced by moderate-to-
high magnitude earthquakes. They discuss the seismogenic source responsible for these seismites,
relating the seismite outcrops in the Baza area to the activity of the BF. However, they suggest
that other seismogenic sources, closer to the Cúllar seismites, could not be dismissed.
In the NE sector of the Baza Subbasin, Gibert et al. (2005) interpreted a series of deformation
structures affecting the Lower Pleistocene lacustrine sediments as a low-angle planar slump
developed in a shallow subaqueous deltaic environment. The authors proposed that the probable
trigger mechanism was an earthquake (or series of earthquakes).
More recently, Alfaro et al. (2010) described new outcrops of giant seismites in Plio-Pleistocene
sediments, with a significant lateral continuity in different sectors of the subbasin (Galera, Cúllar
and N of Baza) (Fig. 18). In this work, they analysed the sedimentary conditions and physical
characteristics that favoured the formation and preservation of these deformation features.
Based on the distribution of these structures, they suggested the BF and the GF as the most
likely seismogenic sources for the events that formed these seismites, concluding that moderate
earthquakes, with maximum magnitudes of approximately 7.0, could have produced them.

2.2.6.3. Instrumental seismicity

The seismicity belonging to the historical period has an asymmetrical spatial distribution in
the Guadix-Baza Basin, as most of the earthquakes occurred in the Baza Subbasin (Fig. 17)
(Martinez-Solares and Mezcua, 2002; Martinez-Solares and Olivera Serrano, 2007; Sanz de
Galdeano et al., 2012). The seismicity in the Baza Subbasin is characterised by shallow events of
low to moderate magnitude (Fig. 19).
The maximum registered magnitude event during the instrumental period was the 1964 Galera
Earthquake (I= VIII, mbLg 4.7, Martínez-Solares and Mezcua, 2002; Silva et al., 2019). The
ground shaking produced by this event caused severe damage to many buildings in Galera, Orce,
Huéscar, and Castilléjar towns (Silva et al., 2019). This earthquake did not caused casualties,
but it led to a great social alarm. In addition, the 1964 event partially reactivated a previous
massive rock avalanche located to the West of Galera town (Fig. 20). The GF has been proposed
as the seismogenic source of this event (García-Tortosa et al., 2007). These authors also discussed
whether the 1973 Huéscar Earthquake (mbLg 4.0) and the relatively high level of background
seismicity in the area could be assigned to the GF or to the Alfahuara-Botardo structure. Other
remarkable events include the 1962 SE Caniles (mbLg 4.7) and the 2003 Benamaurel (mbLg 4.1)
earthquakes (Martínez-Solares and Mezcua, 2002) that were related to the BF. The 1979 Cúllar
earthquake (mbLg 4.2, Martínez-Solares and Mezcua, 2002) seems to have been associated with
the Cúllar faults, as inferred exclusively from geophysical data (Sanz de Galdeano et al., 2007a
and 2012). Apart from these moderate-magnitude events, low-magnitude earthquake (mbLg 1.0
to 1.3) clusters were registered close to Benamaurel town in 2004. The only focal mechanism
available in the Baza Subbasin is the mechanism calculated for the 2003 Benamaurel (mbLg 4.1)
Earthquake by the Instituto Andaluz de Geofísica (Fig. 19). This focal mechanism is located
close to the BF and reflects pure normal faulting (Stich et al. 2006), with roughly ENE-WSW
oriented T-axes, which are coherent with the fault structure and kinematics.

Fig. 20. A) Metric ground fissures and social alarm caused by the 1964 Galera Earthquake (front page of the
ABC newspaper, 10th June 1964). B) Massive rock avalanche west of Galera town, partially reactivated during
the 1964 Galera earthquake.

CHAPTER 3

METHODOLOGY

<!-- p. 39 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

\3. Methodology

To achieve the objectives proposed in this PhD thesis, we apply a methodology based on the
characterisation of active faults via a multidisciplinary approach. We integrate stratigraphic,
structural, tectono-geomorphological, geodetic, and seismological data. In the following sections,
we discuss the different techniques we use to reach the goals of this work.

3.1. Structural characterisation

Previously to characterise an active fault from a structural point of view, it was necessary to
define its geological framework. This process starts with a stratigraphic characterization in
the fault environment, including a description of the different types of rocks, their ages, and
their spatial distribution. The aim of this characterisation is to identify possible stratigraphic
markers that can be used to estimate the fault throw between different fault blocks. Moreover,
the stratigraphic characterisation also permits the detection of potential tectonic control on the
sedimentary environments within the study areas. Therefore, in this PhD thesis, we define a
series of informal lithostratigraphic units for both the Baza and Galera regions. The age of these
units is based on previous stratigraphic and palaeontological studies.
The structural characterisation of the studied active faults is performed at different scales: the
description of the surface fault geometry and the kinematic analysis are carried out at the map
and outcrop scales, while analysis of the internal structure of the fault zone is conducted at the
outcrop scale.
For the description of the fault array at different scales, we consider the basic model of a fault
zone (Fig. 21), which includes two main architectural elements: the fault core and the damage
zone (Caine et al., 1996). The fault core accommodates most of the fault displacement and
strain and is composed of fault rocks (Braathen et al., 2009; Gabrielsen et al., 2017; Torabi et al.,
2019), one or multiple slip surfaces (Caine et al., 1996), and/or clay/shale smears (Vrolijk et al.,
\2016) that may have undergone structural diagenesis (Eichhubl et al., 2005 and 2009; Laubach
et al., 2010; Solum et al., 2010; Sánchez-Roa et al., 2016). The damage zone is made up of
secondary structures, such as smaller faults, folds, fractures, and/or deformation bands (Shipton
and Cowie, 2001 and 2003; Kim et al., 2004; Fossen et al., 2005). In poorly lithified sediments,
mixing of sediments can occur in the fault zone, forming a “mixed zone” located between the
fault core and the damage zone (Heynekamp et al., 1999; Rawling and Goodwin, 2006; Loveless
et al., 2011; Braathen et al., 2013).

76 77

<!-- p. 40 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

fault scarps) record offsets between 1 and tens of metres. With these criteria, two groups of fault
strands have been defined: i) block-bounding faults or major faults with larger displacements,
i.e., large enough to juxtapose stratigraphic units with an age difference of at least 1 Ma and/or
with a geomorphic expression along the mountain front that is several tens of metres high, and
ii) intrablock faults, secondary faults, or minor faults with minor displacements, i.e., not large
enough to juxtapose stratigraphic units with an age difference of at least 1 Ma, and a geomorphic
expression in the form of fault scarps that are a few metres high or less (no scarps). This is easily
applicable in the BF, as it is a normal fault. However, the GF is a fault with a main strike-
slip component. This, together with the predominantly flat geometry of the stratigraphic units,
make it impossible to define major faults from these criteria. Therefore, in the GF we consider
the main fault strands are those that present the same orientation as the overall fault system, i.e.,
faults subparallel to the main slip direction. Consequently, these strands may present the higher
horizontal displacement.
The subsurface characterisation of the faults, i.e., the deep geometry and total vertical cumulative
throw, is reconstructed using the available gravity survey carried out by Alfaro et al. (2008).
These authors calculate the residual anomaly by subtracting the regional from the Bouguer
anomaly. We also use available seismic reflection surveys (Haberland et al., 2017) to support
fault subsurface characterisation. Thus, gravity and seismic profiles are georeferenced together
with our surface data in structural 3D modelling software (MOVE ©, developed by Petroleum
Experts, PETREX) to interpolate the 3D geometry of the fault and of the top surface of the Baza
Subbasin basement.

2019).

Evidence of palaeoearthquakes can be primary or secondary. Primary evidence consists of features
caused directly by a surface-rupturing event, i.e., displacement and/or deformation at or near
the ground surface. Such evidence includes offset or folded stratigraphic or geomorphic markers,

3.4. Palaeoseismological analysis

Knowing whether a fault hosted high-magnitude events in the past is important for the
seismogenic characterisation of an active fault. It is well known that the farther the seismic record
can be extended back in time, the more realistic the seismic hazard assessments will be, especially
for slow faults with long recurrence intervals. In these cases, seismic hazard assessments based
exclusively on historic and instrumental records present limited reliability, and they must be

90 91

Fig. 29. Preliminary interpretation (logs), over the high resolution photomosaics, of the structural and

Galera Fault.

Fig. 28. A) Excavation of palaeoseismological trenches crossing the GF. B) Mapping of faults and stratigraphic
horizons exposed on the trench wall surface using coloured pins.

The proposed palaeo-events are dated using several radiocarbon and OSL samples. Samples of
charcoal represent the main issue of the interpreted rupture history, as the sample age characterises
a maximum constraint for the containing unit. Continental gastropod shell samples are often
avoided for 14C dating because many taxa incorporate “dead” carbon from carbonate rocks when
building their shells. This phenomenon, referred to as the “Limestone Problem” (Goodfriend
and Stipp, 1983), can cause the radiocarbon ages of gastropod shells to be as much as ca. 3,000
yr too old. Despite this, we include several dated snail samples in our analysis, but instead of the
analytical error, an error of 3,200 yr is considered for these samples.

This software uses Okada’s approach (Okada, 1985 and 1992) to model the deformation of an
elastic half-space with constant elastic properties. The fault is modelled as several fault planes,
approximating as much as possible the geometry of the BF. Given the geometry (total length,
dip and seismogenic depth) of the fault, the Mmax magnitude is modelled using a uniform
displacement according to the seismic moment corresponding to the Mmax event (sensu Wells
and Coppersmith, 1994). A transition zone was used at the edges of the fault to avoid significant

CHAPTER 4

THE BAZA FAULT

<!-- p. 53 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

\4. The Baza Fault

This section is focused on characterising the active BF. First, we describe the stratigraphic
framework of the fault to identify the offset materials. By means of a structural characterisation,
based on both surface and subsurface data, we define the geometry (length, dip, strike, and
width) and kinematics of the fault system. Furthermore, we identify, describe and discuss the
along-strike variations in these parameters (published in Medina-Cascales et al., 2020). From
new GPS data, we constrain short-term fault slip rates for the BF, and we compare them with
the long-term geological rates proposed by previous studies (published in Alfaro et al., 2021).
We also carry out an outcrop-scale 3D characterisation of the fault zone architecture. For this
purpose, we describe and interpret a large (ca. 15×15×4 m) trench (including 13 sections)
excavated in one of the main strands of the BF. An analysis of the distribution of deformation
and deformation styles allows us to assess the fault zone evolution (published in Medina-Cascales
et al., 2019).
We present the first palaeoseismological study of the BF, including a preliminary surface rupturing
history and recurrence intervals of the identified palaeoearthquakes (published in Castro et al.,
2018). Finally, we use the geometric, kinematic, and palaeoseismological data obtained for the
BF to characterise its seismogenic potential and segmentation, defining its maximum expected
magnitude, mean recurrence times, and a geodetic rupture scenario (published in Medina-
Cascales et al., 2020).

4.1. Stratigraphic framework of the Baza Fault

Since the Late Miocene, the activity of the BF has controlled the sedimentary environments in the
Guadix-Baza Basin, juxtaposing different facies that crop out in the different fault blocks (Alfaro
et al., 2008; García-Tortosa et al., 2008b) (Figs. 35 and 36). In addition, the development of a
lake in the Baza Subbasin during Plio-Pleistocene times resulted in a concentric distribution of
sedimentary environments, leading to lateral changes in facies from the margins to the subbasin
depocentre, mainly developed on the BF hanging wall (Torres et al., 2003; Gibert et al., 2007a).
The basement of the Baza Subbasin is highly heterogeneous, as it consists of three different
tectono-stratigraphic domains. The basement to the south of Baza town consists of Triassic
metamorphic rocks of the Alpujárride Complex (Betic Internal Zones, Figs. 35 and 36) (e.g.,
García-Dueñas et al., 1992; Orozco and Alonso-Chaves, 2002). This complex is formed by
several stacked tectonic units bounded by major thrusts that were reactivated as low-angle

104 105

<!-- p. 54 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

normal faults (e.g., García Dueñas et al., 1992; Orozco and Alonso-Chaves, 2002). Each of
these tectonic units consists of a basal meta-detrital formation (alternating phyllites, mica schists
and marbles) and an overlying meta-carbonate formation (dolostones and limestones) (Delgado,
1978; Delgado et al., 1980; Martin-Rojas et al., 2009 and 2012). Consequently, this basement
presents a multilayer pattern with high mechanical contrast, as weak and strong layers alternate.
The Alpujárride basement crops out on the southern margin of the Baza Subbasin (Fig. 35).
The basement between the area north of Baza town and south of the Negratín Reservoir is
made up of a massive and homogeneous Jurassic carbonate succession belonging to the Dorsal
Units (Durand-Delga and Foucault, 1968) or Frontal Units (Martín-Algarra et al., 2004) of the
Betic Internal Zones (Figs. 35 and 36). The basement extending to the north of the Negratín
Reservoir is formed by a sedimentary succession of Mesozoic carbonates and marls belonging to
the Subbetic and Prebetic Domains of the Betic External Zones (Fig. 36) (Azéma et al., 1979;
García-Hernández et al., 1980).
Lying unconformably on top of this basement is the sedimentary infill of the Baza Subbasin. We
describe below the lithostratigraphic units we define in the Baza region.
The oldest unit of the sedimentary infill is unit 1 (Figs. 35 and 36), which lies unconformably
on top of the basement. Unit 1 is composed of conglomerates, reef limestones, sands and marls.
These are interpreted as Upper Miocene (uppermost Tortonian, ca. 8 Ma) fan delta and shallow
marine deposits (Guerra-Merchán, 1992; García-García et al, 2006).
After the onset of normal faulting, the sedimentary environments were controlled by the BF
during the Pliocene and Pleistocene. Moreover, the surface expression of the BF consists of a
fault array whose width and number of fault strands vary along strike. Fault strands with larger
displacements within this fault array define a footwall block, a central block and a hanging
wall block. Lithostratigraphic units deposited during the endorheic stage can be separated into
three groups according to their positions within these blocks of the BF: i) units deposited in
the footwall; ii) units deposited in the central block (fault zone); and iii) units deposited in the
hanging wall (Fig. 36).

Footwall block units

The footwall of the BF (Figs. 35 and 36) is dominated in the SW of the study area by an extensive
unit made up of massive conglomerates with heterometric metamorphic clasts supported by a
sandy matrix (unit 2, Figs. 36 and 37A). This unit is separated by unconformities with both
the basement and unit 1. Unit 2 is interpreted as upper Miocene to lower Pliocene debris flow
facies deposited in the proximal part of an alluvial fan. The source area of the clasts in unit 2
was the S margin of the basin (Goy et al., 1989; Guerra-Merchán, 1992; García-García et al.,
2006). To the N, unit 2 laterally grades to unit 3 (Fig. 36). This latter unit is composed of marls
and limestones interbedded with silts (Fig. 37B). Unit 3 is interpreted as Pliocene (ca. 5 to 2.8
Ma) marginal lacustrine deposits (Guerra-Merchán et al., 1991; Guerra-Merchán, 1992; RosMontoya et al., 2017). To the N of Baza town, unit 3 grades into unit 4 and is overlain by unit 9
(Fig. 36). Unit 4 is made up of greyish and reddish silts and sands with interbedded fine-grained
carbonates (Fig. 37C). Unit 9 consists of greenish and dark grey sands, sandy silts and black clay
levels (Fig. 37H). Locally, it is possible to recognize bodies of fine gravels and sands, including
clasts and grains, from the Internal Zones of the Betic Cordillera (quartzite, mica schist, etc.). Fig. 35. Geological map of the Baza Fault and Baza sector. Red lines show the location of the geological cross
sections in figure 39.

106 107

<!-- p. 55 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

Hanging wall block units

The hanging wall of the BF is dominated in the S by units 5 and 10 (Figs. 35 and 36). Unit
10 consists of sandy marls, silts, sands and conglomerate deposits (Fig. 37I) with fine and
discontinuous nodular carbonate levels. It is also very common to observe discontinuous levels
with high concentrations of Cerastoderma. This unit is interpreted as Pliocene to Pleistocene
terminal alluvial fan deposits (ca. 0.8 Ma for the top of unit 10; Guerra-Merchán and Ruíz-
Bustos, 1992). In the northernmost part of the study area, unit 8 crops out (Figs. 35 and 36).
This unit is formed by yellowish sandy silts, sands, and lutites (Fig. 37G). This unit is interpreted
as Pliocene to Lower Pleistocene, transitional alluvial to lacustrine facies (ca. 2.5 to 2 Ma, Torres
et al., 2003). To the S and to the N, units 8 and 10 change laterally into unit 11, respectively
(Figs. 35 and 36). Unit 11 is made up of interbedded carbonate silts, dark marls and gypsum
levels (Fig. 37J). This unit corresponds to Pliocene to Pleistocene shallow lacustrine deposits
(ca. 1.2 to 0.7 Ma, Gibert et al., 2007a). Units 8 and 11 change laterally into the central part
of the Baza Subbasin to unit 12 (Fig. 36). This unit consists of interbedded carbonate silts with
abundant fine-grained laminated gypsum (Fig. 37K). Unit 12 is interpreted as Lower to Middle
Pleistocene (ca. 2.1 to 0.7 Ma at the top) distal lacustrine deposits from the palaeolake inner
zone (Gibert et al., 2007a and 2007b).
Overlying these endorheic units, Pleistocene-Holocene sediments (unit 13, Fig. 36), such as
colluvial slope deposits, travertines, floodplain deposits, and fluvial terraces (Fig. 37L), were
deposited during the exorheic stage of the basin. Some of these recent deposits are offset by
the BF.Fig. 36. Simplified 3D scheme showing the spatial distribution and correlation of the defined lithostratigraphic
units along the Baza sector. Same unit names and numbers as in Fig. 35. EZ: External Zones.

Units 4 and 9 are interpreted as Pliocene to Lower Pleistocene (ca. 4 to 2 Ma; Agustí et al., 2001)
distal fluvio-alluvial deposits belonging to the fluvial system developed in the Guadix Subbasin.
In the northernmost part of the footwall block, unit 6 crops out (Fig. 36). This unit is made
up of white clays and conglomerates (6a) that laterally change to the S to alternating yellowish
sandy silts, clays, conglomerates and limestone levels (6b, Fig. 37E). Unit 6 is interpreted as
Pliocene to Lower Pleistocene distal alluvial fan deposits (unit 6, Fig. 36), whose source area is
the N margin of the basin (Vera, 1970b).

Central block units

The central block is dominated by units 2 and 5 to the south (Figs. 35 and 36). Unit 5 grades
laterally from unit 2 and consists of reddish conglomerates with metamorphic clasts interbedded
with sandstones (Fig. 37D). This unit is interpreted as upper Miocene to Lower Pleistocene
alluvial fan deposits coming from the S margin of the basin (Guerra-Merchán, 1992). To the
north, this unit changes laterally to units 3, 7 and 9 (Fig. 36). Unit 7 crops out in Baza town
and is composed of layered grey silts with marl levels (Fig. 37F). The upper part of this unit is
formed by white and reddish cross-bedded conglomerates and microconglomerates. Unit 7 is
interpreted as Lower Pleistocene (ca. 2.5 Ma) marginal lacustrine deposits (Guerra-Merchán et
al., 1991; Guerra-Merchán and Ruíz-Bustos, 1992; Peña, 1979). In the northernmost part of
the central block, only unit 9 is present (Figs. 35 and 36).

and marls of unit 6. F) Laminated silts of unit 7. Fig. 37 b. G) Sandy silts and sandstones of unit 8. H) Dark silts and clays of unit 9. I) Dark sands and

4.2. Baza Fault geometry

In the following sections, we describe the BF surface and subsurface geometry, i.e., length,
dip, strike, width, and fault terminations. We define the structure of the fault zone and the
damage zone, defining fault sectors based on their surface geometry. We also describe alongstrike geometric variations and discuss them in terms of basement heterogeneities, orientation
of the fault with respect to the regional extension, and interaction with other faults.

4.2.1. Geometry of the Baza Fault

The BF is an approximately 40 km-long structure, that strikes NNW-SSE to NW-SE, and that
crosses almost the entire Baza Subbasin (Figs. 35 and 38). The BF presents a curved trace in map
view. We divide the BF into two sectors based on the surface geometry of the fault array (Fig.
38). The 19 km-long northern sector strikes NNW-SSE, and its width varies between 40 and
1,000 m. The southern sector strikes NW-SE, is 21 km long, and has a width varying between
1 and 7 km (Figs. 38 and 39).
The surface expression of the BF varies along strike. It consists of a set of closely spaced faults,
i.e., interconnected fault strands, whose number ranges from only 1 strand in the N to more
than 10 strands in the S. We identify three block-bounding faults: F1, F2, and F3 (Figs. 38 and
39). F1 can be traced along the entire BF. It juxtaposes lithostratigraphic units with ages of 4 Ma
and 2.5 Ma (Fig. 39), generating a mountain front up to approximately 100 m high (Fig. 40).
Moreover, available seismic profiles (Haberland et al., 2017) show that F1 is the Baza halfgraben boundary fault to the west (Fig. 15). Consequently, we consider F1 to be the fault with
the largest displacement in the entire BF system. F2 crops out only in the northern sector of
the BF, while F3 appears only in the southern sector. F2 and F3 juxtapose lithostratigraphic
units with ages of ca. 2.5 and 1 Ma and produce a small mountain front a few tens of metres
high (up to approximately 25 m). In addition, seismic profiles show that F2 and F3 present less
displacement than F1. We interpret F2 and F3 as major fault splays of fault F1. F1, F2 and F3
define the three main fault blocks previously mentioned (Figs. 38 and 39): i) The footwall of the
BF is located west of F1; ii) A central block is located between F1 and F2 in the northern sector
and between F1 and F3 in the southern sector. This central block corresponds to the fault zone
of the BF; iii) The hanging wall of the BF is located east of F2 and F3. We consider the rest of
the BF strands to be secondary faults, i.e., intrablock faults that internally deform these three
main blocks (Fig. 38).

Fig. 38. Surface geometry map of the Baza Fault. Brown shading represents the main fault bends and relay
zones. Green shading represents the relay zone between the F2 and F3 faults. Dark red blobs represent towns.
In this map, stratigraphy is simplified by age.

4.2.1.1. Northern sector of the BF

In the northern sector, the fault zone of the BF is bounded by F1 and F2, and deformation is Fig. 41. Close-ups of the Baza Fault traces A) Map showing the location of the close-ups in B to D. B), C) and
concentrated along a narrow damage zone (from 40 to 1,000 m wide) (Figs. 38, 39, and 40). In D) Close-ups showing the main fault bends and in the northern sector of the BF. E) Sketch of the structural
this northern sector, the overall strike of the BF grades from NNW-SSE (approximately N175E) features of a breached relay ramp.
to N-S (Fig. 38).
A closer look reveals that fault strands in the northern sector present local strike variations. These
strike variations define fault bends extending tens to hundreds of metres along F1 (e.g., R1 in
Fig. 41B and R4 in Fig. 41D) and F2 (e.g., R2 and R3 in Fig. 41C). Locally, in these areas, beds
gently dip perpendicular to the fault bends (e.g., Figs. 41B and D). Intrablock faults striking
parallel to the fault bends are also observed (Fig. 41B). Other intrablock faults extend out of the
fault bend striking parallel to the strike of the block-bounding faults (Figs. 41B and 41C).

116 117

<!-- p. 60 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

Fig. 42. F1 as the single block-bounding fault in the northernmost part of the Baza Fault northern sector.
Location in section I-I’ in Fig. 39.

These structural features indicate that these fault bends are breached relay ramps developed by
hard-linked overlapping fault segments (Fig. 41E): the beds dipping perpendicular to the fault
bends represent ramp-like folds, the intrablock faults striking parallel to the fault bends conform
to the damage zone resulting after deformation within the relay ramp, and the intrablock faults
extending out of the fault bends represent the remains of the original linked fault segments. R1,
R2 and R4 are single-tip relay ramps, while R3 is a doubly breached relay ramp (sensu Fossen
and Rotevatn, 2016).
Along-strike fault bends lead to width variations in the surface expression of the fault zone. In
the northernmost part of the BF, the fault zone is only a few metres wide, as only one blockbounding fault (F1) is recognized (I-I’ in Fig. 39 and Fig. 42). Southwards, F2 branches out
from F1 (Fig. 41B); this feature, combined with a right bend in F1, produces a widening of the
fault zone up to approximately 200 m (II-II’ in Fig. 39).
The fault zone width decreases southwards down to 40-100 m (III-III’ in Fig. 39 and Fig. 43)
as a consequence of a left bend in F1. After that, successive right bends in both F1 and F2
southwards widen the fault zone up to 600 m (IV-IV’ and V-V’ in Fig. 39). In addition to the
F1 and F2 block-bounding faults, the damage zone in the northern sector of the BF is composed
of a variable number of interconnected intrablock faults. These are more abundant (a total of
9 distinct secondary fault strands, at map scale) along the southern part of the northern sector, Fig. 43. A) Uninterpreted and B) interpreted Baza Fault zone in the northern sector. Location in section III-III’ in
leading to an approximately 1 km-wide damage zone (V-V’ in Fig. 39 and Fig. 41D). figure 39 C) Close-up of the inner fault zone structure of F1 fault. D) Close up of the inner fault zone structure
of F2 fault.

118 119

<!-- p. 61 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

4.2.1.2. Northern termination

As described above, along the northern termination of the BF, F1 and F2 join approximately 4
km south of the fault tip (Fig. 39B), and displacement concentrates on a single block-bounding
fault (F1) (Fig. 42). At the northern termination of the BF, the fault strike changes from NNWSSE (N175E) to SW-NE (N30E) (Fig. 44A). In this area, the geomorphic expression of the
BF is a subtle 1-2 m-high scarp. In addition, in this northern termination, the BF juxtaposes
stratigraphic units with analogous ages (units 6b and 8, Pliocene-Early Pleistocene, I-I’ in Fig.
39 and Fig. 44B). In this northernmost part of the BF, beds in the hanging wall gently dip to
the south (approximately 5-10°), parallel to the fault trace (Fig. 44A). At the very end of the
northern termination, the F1 fault splays into multiple secondary fault strands, leading to an
approximately 800 m-wide damage zone. The northern tip of the BF intersects another fault that
extends eastwards striking N70E, subparallel to the GF (Fig. 44C). In this area, minor SW-NE
strike-slip faults crop out (I-I’ in Fig. 39 and Fig. 45).

Fig. 45. Strike-slip fault showing a pop-up structure close to the Baza Fault northern termination. Location in
section I-I’ in figure 39.

4.2.1.3. Southern sector of the BF

In the transition between the northern and southern sectors, F2 coalesces with F1 (Fig. 46B). The
northern tip point of the F3 fault is located approximately 500 m north of this coalescence point;
consequently, F2 and F3 partially overlap (R5 in Fig. 46B). This overlap is an approximately 500
m-wide and 1,000 m-long area where bedding gently dips to the north, parallel to the BF strike.
To the south, F3 accommodates a significant proportion of the total offset of the BF, juxtaposing
units with significantly different ages (Plio-Pleistocene, ca. 2.5 Ma deposits of unit 7 against
Pleistocene, ca. 1 Ma deposits of unit 11; VI-VI’ in Fig. 39). Consequently, in this southern
sector, the fault zone of the BF is bounded by F1 to the W (Fig. 47) and F3 to the E (Fig. 38 and
VI-VI’ to VIII-VIII’ in Fig. 39). The strike of the BF changes along the southern sector, grading
southwards from N165E to N135E. Furthermore, in this sector, deformation is distributed in a
wide damage zone that gradually widens southwards from 1 to 7 km (Fig. 38).

Fig. 44. A) Close-up of the Baza Fault northern termination. B) Northernmost outcrop of the F1 fault. C) Striking
N70E, S dipping normal fault that intersects the Baza Fault in its northern termination.

120 121

<!-- p. 62 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

Again, a closer look reveals that fault strands in the southern sector present strike variations.
Along-strike fault bends extending hundreds to thousands of metres are also present along the
trace of F1 (e.g., R6 and R7 in Fig. 46B; R8 and R10 in Fig. 46C). These variations define the
trace of F1 formed by alternate portions striking approximately N165E and N135E (Figs. 38
and 46C). Additionally, fault bends tens to hundreds of metres long characterise F3 (e.g., R11
and R12 in Fig. 46C). All these bends are responsible for the curved traces of faults F1 and F3
in map view. Intrablock faults striking parallel to the fault bends are observed (e.g., R7 in Fig.
46B). In addition, intrablock faults extend out of the fault bend striking parallel to the strike of
the block-bounding faults (e.g., R7 in Fig. 46B, and R8 and R11 in Fig. 46C). Thus, R7, R8,
R10, R11, and R12 are single-tip relay ramps, while R6 is a doubly breached relay ramp (sensu
Fossen and Rotevatn, 2016).
Successive right fault bends and overlaps are responsible for the gradual southward widening of
the fault zone in the southern sector (Fig. 38). To the north of Baza town, the overlap between
F2 and F3 produces an approximately 600 m-wide fault zone (R5 in Fig. 46B and VI-VI’ and
VII-VII’ in Fig. 39). Southwards, right bends along F1 produce a widening of the fault zone up
to approximately 3 km (R6 and R7 in Fig. 46B) and even up to 4 km (R8 to R12 in Fig. 46C
and VIII-VIII’ in Fig. 39).
In addition to the F1 and F3 block-bounding faults, the damage zone in the southern sector of
the BF is composed of a large number of subparallel intrablock faults. These faults appear mainly
in the central block and in the hanging wall (Fig.48). Intrablock faults are more abundant to the
south (with 14 observable secondary fault strands), leading to a maximum damage zone width
of 7 km (Fig. 38 and VII-VII’ in Fig. 39). Many of these faults are not interconnected at the
surface, so faults are soft-linked by means of fault overlap.

4.2.1.4. Southern termination

Displacement at the southern termination of the BF is distributed into two block-bounding
faults (F1 and F3) and several intrablock faults (Fig. 49A).
At the southern termination, F1 presents a geomorphic expression defined by a mountain front
approximately 25 m high and juxtaposes upper Miocene (Tortonian) unit 1 in the footwall and
upper Miocene-Pliocene unit 2 (Fig. 49B and VIII-VIII’ in Fig. 39) in the hanging wall. To the
SE, the geomorphic expression of F1 is subtler (approximately 2 m-high scarp), and the fault
juxtaposes unit 2 in both the footwall and hanging wall. Farther to the SE, F1 splays into several
minor fractures (Fig. 49A). F3 also shows a prominent geomorphic expression (an approximately
20 m-high mountain front). This geomorphic expression gradually becomes subtler southwards.
Fault F3 juxtaposes the same units in both the footwall and the hanging wall (Fig. 49C, locally,
F3 juxtaposes units 1 and 5 because unit 5 is only approximately 5 m thick).

Fig. 47. A) F1 block bounding fault cropping out in the Baza Fault southern sector. B) Close-up and interpretation
of F1 fault zone inner structure. Location in section VII-VII’ in figure 39.
