4.2.1.5. Fault-related folds

At the regional scale, the displacement of the BF causes a gentle regional tilt to the W in the
hanging wall (ca. 5°) (Fig. 50A) (Figs. 7 and 8 in Haberland et al., 2017), leading to a typical
tilted block/half graben structure (Leeder and Gawthorpe, 1987) related to a roll-over anticline.
This tilting can also be observed in the glacis surface (García-Tortosa et al., 2008b and 2011a).
The northernmost sector of the BF is characterised by S-SE tilting (Fig. 50B), related to the
gradual decrease in displacement from the central part of the BF towards its tip.

Fig. 50. A) Panoramic view showing the westward tilting of the glacis surface in the Baza Subbasin. B)
Southward tilting of the Baza Fault hanging wall close to the northern fault termination.

At more detailed scale, our detailed field study and structural analysis reveal the existence of
minor folds related to the BF (Figs. 51 and 52). Most of these fault-related folds are better
exposed in the northern sector and in the eastern sector of the southern sector of the BF, as they
are especially abundant in areas where fine-grained rocks crop out (e.g., units 3, 4, 7, or 8 to 11).
Fault drag geometries occur close to the BF trace (Fig. 51). These folds are asymmetric, with
metre- to decametre-scale wavelengths, and their axial traces are sub-parallel to the fault traces
in map view. They are represented both as footwall and hanging wall monoclines and synclines.
Although these structures are commonly interpreted as a result of frictional sliding along the
fault, the mechanism producing these folds is more likely related to extensional fault-propagation
folding (Ferrill et al., 2005 and 2012; Smart et al., 2009 and 2010; Hardy and Allmendinger,
2011).
In some sectors, gentle roughly symmetric folds appear between secondary strands in both the
central and eastern main blocks. Their wavelengths are a few tens of metres and their hinges are
sub-parallel to fault traces. Similar structures are observed on seismic profiles at the regional scale
(Fig. 8 in Haberland et al., 2017), related to fault splays at depth.

SE, intersecting the F2 fault trace obliquely.

4.2.1.6. Subsurface structure

The general arrangement of the BF at depth is equivalent to the surface expression, as it consists
of a northern sector striking NNW-SSE and a southern sector striking NW-SE (Fig. 53A). In
the seismic profiles (Haberland et al., 2017), the BF presents a listric geometry in section, with a
dip ranging between 45 and 60° (with an overall value of 55°) to the east. The total cumulative
throw of the BF can be determined from the available residual gravity anomaly survey (Alfaro
et al., 2008) (Fig. 53B). The marker used for this calculation is the modelled contact between
the basement and the sedimentary infill of the subbasin, considering that this surface was at
the same height before the onset of the deformation related to the BF (Alfaro et al., 2008). In
this PhD thesis we conduct an analysis of this dataset in terms of throw variation along the BF.
This analysis (Fig. 53C) indicates that the throw increases southwards along the northern sector,
from 0 m in the northern tip to approximately 2,400 m in its central part. From this area, the
total throw decreases towards the transition zone between the northern and southern sectors
(approximately 2,000 m). Along the southern sector, the cumulative throw increases southwards
to maximum values of approximately 2,400 m and then decreases to approximately 400 m close
to the southern tip of the BF. The maximum displacements of the BF in the central parts of
the northern and southern sectors (Fig. 53C) are responsible for two depocentres in the Baza
Subbasin.

that the subsurface orientation of the BF is NNW-SSE in the northern sector and NW-SE in the southern sector.

4.2.2. Along-strike geometric variations of the fault zone

As we described previously, we recognize two sectors in the BF according to differences in the
surface geometry of the fault array (Fig. 38): a northern sector striking N-S to NNW-SSE
(N175E), characterised by a narrow damage zone (maximum width of 1 km) and a southern
sector that strikes NW-SE (N165E to N135E), characterised by a wide damage zone (maximum
width of 7 km). The integration of detailed surface geological observations together with the
available geophysical data allows us to interpret the causes of the along-strike geometric variations
in the BF. Therefore, we interpret the two distinct sectors of the BF as the consequence of three
factors: (1) a heterogeneous basement, (2) the orientation of the BF with respect to the regional
extension, and (3) the interaction with other active faults.

4.2.2.1. Heterogeneous basement

Structural and stratigraphic basement heterogeneity in regions undergoing extension determines
the surface expression of faults. A normal fault offsetting a heterogeneously layered sequence
with competence contrast develops a stepped geometry, as the fault presents a steeper dip in
levels with higher friction angles (Childs et al., 1996; Schmatz et al., 2010).
The BF offsets a heterogeneous basement formed by several major tectonic domains (DurandDelga and Foucault, 1968; Delgado, 1978; Delgado et al., 1980; Azéma et al., 1979; GarcíaHernández et al., 1980). The northern sector of the BF offsets the massive limestone succession
belonging to the Dorsal domain (also referred to as the Frontal Units) of the Betic Internal Zones
(Durand-Delga and Foucault, 1968; Martín-Algarra et al., 2004) (Figs. 35 and 36). As this is
a mechanically homogeneous basement, the fault is more likely to present a homogeneous dip
geometry (with no major along-dip bends). Consequently, a narrow damage zone is developed at
the surface (Ferrill et al., 2017) (Fig. 54A). The presence of the Jabalcón Peak structural high may
also constrain the BF geometry, leading to an even narrower fault zone in this area (Fig. 38 and
III-III’ in Fig. 39). The southern sector of the BF offsets basement belonging to the Alpujárride
Complex (Figs. 35 and 36). This basement consists of alternating mechanically heterogeneous
layers, so it is characterised by competence contrasts (e.g., Delgado, 1978; García-Dueñas et al.,
1992; Orozco and Alonso-Chaves, 2002). As a result, an along-dip stepped geometry is expected
for the BF in this area (Ferrill et al., 2017) (Fig. 54A). These fault steps represent asperities with
respect to fault movement, resulting in extensive hanging wall strain that forms contractional
or extensional areas in which fault networks develop and splay upwards (e.g., Legg et al., 2007;
Childs et al., 2009; Janecke et al., 2010; Kurt et al., 2013; Peacock and Anderson, 2012). This
led to the formation of the wide damage zone that can be observed at the surface in the BF
southern sector.

Fig. 54 (see next page). A) Schematic sketch representing how the heterogeneous basement of the Baza
Subbasin is responsible for along-strike width variations in the BF. B) Schematic sketch representing how the
orientation of the basement BF with respect to the regional extension controls the strike and orientation of the
fault array on the surface. C) Schematic sketch representing how fault interaction is responsible for along-strike
variations in the strike and width of the BF.

4.2.2.2. Orientation of the basement Baza Fault related to the regional extension

Pre-existing buried faults control the development of fault arrays in the sedimentary cover. The
orientation of the basement faults with respect to the regional stress field controls the surface
geometry of the fault systems when they propagate upwards through the cover (e.g., Bott, 1959;
Saltzer and Polland, 1992; Giba et al., 2012; Jackson and Rotevatn, 2013; Whipp et al., 2013).
The two sectors of the BF present different orientations with respect to regional extension.
Gravity profiles show that the BF at depth consists of a northern sector striking NNW-SSE and
a southern sector striking NW-SE (Fig. 53A). The narrow damage zone observed at the surface
in the northern sector is, therefore, the result of the upward propagation into the sedimentary
cover of the NNW-SSE striking BF in the basement. That is, in this sector, the subsurface BF
strikes subperpendicular to the WSW-ENE regional extension (Galindo-Zaldívar et al., 1993
and 2015; Stich et al., 2006; Serpelloni et al., 2007; Pérez-Peña et al., 2010a). As a result of
this favourable orientation, the surface expression of the BF in the northern sector consists of a
narrow damage zone subparallel to the basement fault (Fig. 54B) (e.g., Jackson and Rotevatn,
2013; Fossen and Rotevatn, 2016). In contrast, the wider damage zone observed in the southern
sector of the BF is the result of the upward propagation into the cover of the NW-SE-striking
basement BF (Fig. 54B). That is, in the southern sector, the subsurface BF is oblique to the
regional WSW-ENE extension. Buried faults striking oblique to the extension direction first
produce individual fault segments in the cover that strike approximately perpendicular to the
extension. More recently, these segments linked up, resulting in a large fault subparallel to the
strike of the basement fault and producing wider damage zones in the cover (Fig. 54B) (e.g.,
Fossen and Rotevatn, 2016; Deng et al., 2017). This can be observed in the southern sector of
the BF, where fault strands are formed by minor portions oriented approximately perpendicular
to the WSW-ENE extension direction (N175E to N165E), joined by segments oblique to the
extension and subparallel to the basement BF (e.g., F1 in Figs. 38 and 46). We postulate that Fig. 55. Geological map displaying the minor NW-SE normal faults outcropping in the Alpujárride Complex
this change in strike could be controlled by pre-existing fractures. To the SE of the study area basement. White arrows represent the WSW-ENE regional extension.
minor NW-SE normal faults are observed in the outcropping Alpujárride Complex basement
(Fig. 55) (Delgado et al., 1980). These basement faults are sub-parallel to the southern sector of

4.2.3. Concluding remarks about the Baza Fault geometry

We define the BF as an approximately 40 km-long structure striking NNW-SSE to NW-SE. The
detailed structural characterisation permits us to consider two different sectors in the BF based
on its surface geometry: i) a northern sector striking N–S to NNW–SSE (N175E) characterised
by a narrow damage zone (maximum width of 1 km); and ii) a southern sector striking NW–
SE (N165E to N135E) characterised by a wide damage zone (maximum width of 7 km) (Figs.
38 and 39). The BF surface geometry is the result of fault linkage, relay formation and relay
breaching (Fig. 41D).
Subsurface data show that the BF presents an analogous geometry at depth and dips to the
east with a listric geometry. These data also reveal a variable total cumulative throw that is
maximum in the central part of both northern and southern sectors (approximately 2,400 m)
and minimum in the transition zone between the north and the south sectors (approximately
2,000 m).
The along-strike geometric variation between the northern and southern sectors is caused by:
i) differences in the basement in the north and south of the Baza Subbasin, controlling the
fault geometry at depth and thus the geometry of the upward-propagated damage zone in the
cover (Fig. 54A); ii) different orientations of the BF in the basement between the northern
and southern sectors with respect to the WSW-ENE regional extension (Fig. 54B); and iii) the
interaction of the BF with other active faults, leading to interaction damage zones near the fault
terminations (Fig. 54C).
These results are a solid basis for quantifying the seismic potential of the BF and thus for future
seismic hazard assessment studies in the Central Betic Cordillera.

4.3. Baza Fault kinematics

In this section, we define the BF kinematics, determining the component of displacement, sense
of displacement, and slip rates. The direction and sense of displacement are determined by the
analysis of kinematic indicators, while slip rates are estimated from geological and geomorphic
markers, together with geodetic data.

4.3.1. Direction and sense of slip

We analyse the BF kinematics (Fig. 56) from mesoscopic structural features, as slickenlines (Fig.
57), joints and veins (Fig. 58), and S-C structures (Fig. 59).
Slickenlines are the most common kinematic indicators observed on the fault slip surfaces along
the BF (Figs. 56 and 57). Slickensides and slickenlines are more abundant in the northern sector
of the BF, as these features are better preserved in fine-grained lithologies (e.g., unit 9). Fig. 56. Structural measurements along the Baza Fault. Stereoplots 1 to 5 show the orientations of fault slip
surfaces and slickenlines. Almost all the observed slickenlines indicate pure dip slip, i.e. a slip direction varying
according to the BF strike. Consequently, the BF presents convergent directions of slip towards the centre of the
Baza Subbasin. White arrows show the mean slip directions and senses of slip.

sector and SW-NE along the southern sector (Fig. 56). This variation implies that the normal BF
presents convergent directions of slip towards the centre of the Baza Subbasin. This convergence
in slip vectors does not represent the overall extension direction along the BF. We interpret this
as an effect produced close to the surface as result of the accommodation of the sedimentary
cover deposits to the higher vertical displacement produced in the central part of the BF. Rare
oblique slickenlines (rake between 40 and 60°, Fig. 57D) are locally observed in the fault bends
(Fig. 57E and F, stereoplot 5 in Fig. 56). These striations indicate oblique kinematics with a
main normal component and a minor left-lateral component.

and/or spatial variations in fault activity or are merely the result of the observational limitations
of the measurement techniques. Discrepancies between geodetic and geologic slip rates have

4.4. Fault zone inner structure

In this section, we present an outcrop-scale characterisation of the inner architecture of the fault
zone of one of the main strands of the BF. By means of a trench study, we describe and interpret a
3D outcrop dataset. As mentioned above, this trench study comprises 13 different trenches that
permit us to construct a 3D model of the fault zone. In this model we analyse the distribution
and styles of deformation within the fault zone, and we discuss its evolution through space and
time.
The site selected for the meso-scale structural analysis is located in the northern sector of
the BF to the north of Baza town (UTM 30S 526386/4145217, Fig. 63A). In this area, the
damage zone is up to 1 km wide and is formed by several NNW-SSE striking strands (Fig.
63B). The fault zone is up to 400 m wide and limited by the F1 and F2 block-bounding faults.
These major faults juxtapose the footwall, central block, and the hanging wall (Fig. 63B). We
investigate a trench excavated along the F2 block-bounding fault (Fig. 63A), which juxtaposes
upper Pliocene-Lower Pleistocene alluvial distal sediments (units 3 and 9) and Lower-Middle
Pleistocene lacustrine deposits (unit 11, Fig. 63).
In the trench area (Fig. 64A), two main normal fault zones are identified: an eastern fault
zone corresponding to F2 (here referred to as the Eastern Fault, EF) and a western fault zone
corresponding to an intrablock fault strand (here referred to as the Western Fault, WF). At the
outcrop-scale, each of these fault zones comprises a fault core surrounded by damage zones.
Together, they subdivide the area into three different blocks characterised by different stratigraphic
units (Fig. 64B, C, and D): i) a western block corresponding to the WF footwall and composed
mainly of Pliocene carbonate silts (top of unit 3, ca. 2.8 Ma); ii) an eastern block corresponding
to the EF hanging wall and consisting of Middle Pleistocene silts and limestones (unit 11, ca. 0.9
Ma); and iii) a middle block corresponding to the WF hanging wall and EF footwall, which is
composed of interlayered Pliocene carbonate silts, clays, sands and gravels (unit 9, ca. 2.8 Ma).
Because there are no common stratigraphic markers among these three blocks, it is not possible
to accurately estimate the throws of both the WF and EF. Hence, according to the age of units
3 to 11, the EF throw is estimated to be on the order of hundreds of metres, and the WF throw
is greater than ten metres (see section 4.2). The WF and EF are approximately 70 m apart in
the northern part of the study area. However, they gradually merge southwards (Fig. 64A and Fig. 63. A) Geologic map of the Baza Fault in the study area. Red line shows the line of the section in B, and the

blocks with respect to the middle block (Fig. 64D), so the term main deformation zone is used area. Left tick labels are metres above sea level.
to refer to the middle block.

68). Deformation increases eastward and is concentrated around the two main faults WF and EF,

these faults show well-developed fault zones consisting of a fault core and a surrounding damage
zone (sensu Caine et al., 1996). In the case of the WF, its fault core is represented by mm- to ca.
20 cm-wide layered fault gouge, layered sand, clay, and micrite breccia (Table 4), as well as horse
H10 in the central sections of the main deformation zone (Fig. 67B). The WF damage zone
consists of two narrow bands developed in the footwall (H5 in the western block) and hanging

wall (H10 in the MDZ), both dipping towards the downthrown side of the fault. These bands behaviour. It is offset by minor faults in the N (Fig. 67A), while it is smeared towards the WF

similar in width (ca. 1-2 m), so the damage zone is almost symmetrical. On the other hand, the
EF has a more complex fault zone. A ca. 1-2.5 m wide band of more intense deformation in the
main deformation zone in contact with the EF is characterised by high-throw faults (F60 to F80),
highly deformed rock bodies (H40 and the eastern set), clay smears, and clay bodies between fault
slip surfaces that we interpret as clay injection structures. We consider this band the EF fault core
(Figs. 67A and 67B). The EF damage zone is represented on the footwall side (main deformation
zone) by a ca. 3-4 m-wide zone in unit 9 bounded by faults F10 to F51 and on the hanging wall
side (eastern block) by a ca. 2 m-wide syncline in unit 11 (Fig. 67). Thus, the EF damage zone is
asymmetrical, with most of the deformation accumulated in the footwall.
In the Y direction, the most remarkable change is the southward narrowing of the main
deformation zone from ca. 7 m wide in the N (Fig. 67A) to ca. 1 m wide in the S (Fig. 67C).
The distribution of deformation in the X direction in the main deformation zone also varies
with location in the Y direction. In the northern trenches, deformation in the X direction is
characterised by western and central less-deformed zones and an eastern highly deformed zone
close to the EF (Fig. 67A). In the central trenches, deformation increases in the west near the
WF (Fig. 67B). The horses in the central set are offset by minor synthetic and antithetic faults,
e.g., F51 (Fig. 70D), which accommodate extension and are responsible for related structures
such as horsts, grabens and domino faults (Fig. 76). The clay-rich basal (subunit 9I) and top
(subunit 9K) boundaries of H61 act as detachment levels, and subunit 9I is stretched into
boudins (Fig. 76). The most significant variation occurs in the southern trenches B0 to B3,
where the deformation increases dramatically, and bedding can barely be recognized (Fig. 67C).
Fault throw also increases in the southern sections for both small faults, such as F30, and large
faults, such as F70 and F80 (Fig. 73).
In the Z direction, deformation heterogeneities in the main deformation zone are related to the
propagation of faults through subunits of different lithologies. Subunits 9E to 9K are arranged
in two slightly consolidated silty carbonate packages (subunits 9E to 9H and subunits 9J to 9K)
separated by clay-rich subunit 9I (Fig. 77A). Faults propagate upwards and downwards through
the silty packages, and they are arrested at clay-rich subunit 9I, where slip is accommodated by
folds near the fault tips (Fig. 77A).
Clay-rich subunits in the main deformation zone present a distinctive deformation style. The
most remarkable subunit is subunit 9I, which develops injection structures along faults and
detachment levels, mostly in the eastern part of the main deformation zone (Fig. 67A and
B). In these injections, 9I looses its internal lamination. Large fault-controlled clay injections,
such as those along F50, F60, or F80 (Figs. 68 and 74), are heterogeneous in the vertical (Z)
direction, as they are more extensive downwards (Fig. 70). In some cases, the deformation is so
intense that subunit 9I is squeezed, ruptured and isolated (Figs. 67B and 76). Here, subunit
9I is deformed by cm-scale faults (Fig. 77B), laterally grading into a chaotic breccia formed
by internally laminated, rotated fragments surrounded by a clayey matrix (Fig. 77C), and the

4.4.4. Interpretation of the 3D structural variability of the fault zone

The interpretation and correlation of the excavated sections and the derived 3D model of the fault
zone provide valuable insight into the variability of fault zone architecture, styles of deformation,
and fault zone evolution. The fault zone is the result of heterogeneous deformation, which
produces an irregular distribution of structures and deformation styles.

4.4.4.1. Interpretation of fault zone architecture

In the fault zone, deformation is concentrated around the main WF and EF strands. The beds in
the footwall and hanging wall damage zones of these faults are synthetic, i.e., they dip towards
the downthrown block. This observation suggests a component of extensional folding associated
with the propagation of these faults (sensu Ferrill et al., 2005). The damage zone of WF is
symmetrical. This symmetry is interpreted as the result of a similar time span of deformation of
units 3 (footwall) and 9 (hanging wall), since these units are similar in age (ca. 2.8 Ma). On the
other hand, the damage zone of EF is asymmetrical, with most of the deformation accumulating
in the footwall (main deformation zone). This geometry can also be interpreted in terms of the
time span of deformation. Unit 9 in the middle block (ca. 2.8 Ma) is older than unit 11 in the
eastern block (ca. 1.2-0.9 Ma). Consequently, unit 9 accumulated deformation over a longer
period, resulting in a mature fault core and a more complex damage zone. The EF fault zone is
thicker and more deformed than the WF fault zone. Since the throw of the EF is approximately
ten times larger than that of the WF, this situation suggests a correlation between fault zone
thickness and throw, as proposed by Evans (1990).

4.4.4.2. Deformation styles

One of the most interesting features of the studied sections is the variety of deformation styles that
is documented at the outcrop scale. Faults record brittle deformation, while ductile deformation
is expressed by folding, smearing, and clay fluidization (leading to total loss of the original clay
internal structure and to clay injections). It is postulated that the heterogeneous distribution
of these different deformation styles was likely controlled by mechanical stratigraphy (Currie
et al., 1962; Ferrill et al., 2017). To test this hypothesis, a comparison is made between the
deformation styles of the different units and their mechanical stratigraphy. Units 3 to 11 consist
of poorly lithified sediments, which were water-saturated when they were deformed (Gibert et

Unit 3, present in the western fault block, is crosscut by cm- to-m-throw faults and deformed by
an anticline that developed in the WF footwall damage zone. Close inspection of the anticline
forelimb reveals that bedding rotation is accommodated by sub-vertical minor (mm-to cm-
throw) faults (Fig. 67). Thus, with the exception of clay smears from subunit 3G in the WF fault
core, unit 3 mostly underwent brittle deformation.

166 167

<!-- p. 85 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

A wide variety of deformation styles are observed in unit 9 of the main deformation zone. In the
northwestern part, a syncline developed in the WF hanging wall damage zone (Fig. 67A). Close
inspection shows that bedding rotation in the western limb of this syncline is accommodated
by minor (mm-to cm-throw) faults. Synthetic and antithetic faults forming horst and graben
structures are also present in the north-central part of the main deformation zone (Fig. 76).
Consequently, the western and central areas of the main deformation zone to the north
experienced mostly brittle deformation. In the eastern part of the main deformation zone near
the EF, faults, bed rotation and thinning, clay smears and injections are all documented (Fig.
67A and B). These different deformation styles are the result of the multi-layered alternation
of beds. Silty layers underwent brittle deformation, while clay-rich layers experienced ductile
deformation.
In the western part of the main deformation zone, the sediments are mostly carbonate silts
interbedded with thin clay levels (9A to 9H) and coarser gravelly subunit 9B (Figs. 65 and 67).
The predominance of silts and gravels led to more brittle deformation, although smears of H10
(including the gravelly sediments of subunit 9B) along the WF (Fig. 67B), clay smears along
minor faults, and minor folds are documented (Figs. 75B and 76).

to seismic activity (Strachan, 2002; García-Tortosa et al., 2011b). Although the Baza Fault is a

Baza Subbasin (Alfaro et al., 1997 and 2010), further research is necessary to understand the

The WF and EF fault zones define the western, middle, and eastern blocks. In the middle becomes thicker to the south because of the amalgamation of the WF and EF fault cores, giving
block (main deformation zone), the WF hanging wall damage zone, EF footwall damage zone rise to a single fault zone. Obliquely (β and γ) oriented faults (e.g., F40 and F50, Table 4) play
and EF fault core coexist (Fig. 79A and B). The combined activity of these faults led to higher an important role in accommodating the interaction of the WF and EF, particularly where these
deformation and consequently to higher development of structures in the main deformation two faults converge, deeper in the section and to the S (Figs. 70 and 79).
zone. Several models for the development of normal fault zones have been proposed (e.g.,
Peacock and Sanderson, 1991 and 1994; Childs et al., 1996 and 2009; Gabrielsen and Clausen,
2001; Kristensen et al., 2008). In general, these models involve three main stages: 1) an initial
stage in which faulting occurs on a series of segments characterised by surface irregularities;
\2) a second stage in which the segments become linked by relay-ramp breaching or bypassing
surface asperities, forming structures such as horses and duplexes; and 3) a final stage in which
these structures are internally deformed, collapsed, and smeared along the fault. According to
Gabrielsen and Clausen (2001), the fault zone widens during stages 1 and 2, while fault zone
thinning occurs in stage 3. In addition, Childs et al. (2009) suggest that fault zone thickness is
strongly influenced in the first stage by the scale of fault segmentation. The main deformation
zone is formed by a complex arrangement of rock bodies bounded by normal faults. Most of
these rock bodies can be considered as horses forming an extensional duplex (sensu Gibbs, 1984;
Childs et al., 1997). Moreover, some of the main horses show minor internal faults (Figs. 67A,
75A and 79C). According to these observations, it is postulated that the main deformation zone
was characterised by a mature stage of development between stages 2 and 3 above, in which
horses were stacked into duplexes and internally deformed. However, our observations point to
a spatial variation in maturity along the Y (N-S) direction. In the northernmost trenches A00 to
A2, some faults, such as F10 and F11, present low angles and flat-ramp geometries, and the rock
bodies are less deformed (I-I′ in Fig. 79C). These fault surface irregularities indicate a less mature
stage in the N, probably early stage 2. Southwards, in trenches A3 to A5, deformation in the
horses increases; for instance, some minor faults, such as F80, offset low-angle structures (II-II′
in Fig. 79C), dividing H70 into new horses. These features are interpreted as the beginning of
asperity bifurcation, occurring during late stage 2. Finally, in the southernmost trenches B1-B3,
horses are intensely deformed and smeared along fault surfaces, suggesting stage 3 fault zone
development (III-III’ in Fig. 79C). Therefore, the main deformation zone becomes more mature
southwards. This effect is strongly related to the convergence of the WF and EF. This spatial
variation in maturity is interpreted as a consequence of fault zone interaction. Two individual
and well-differentiated WF and EF fault zones can be observed ca. 70 m N of the study area
(Fig. 79A). The fault zones are separated by a distance of ca. 70 m, and each zone features a fault

170 171

<!-- p. 87 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

4.4.5. Concluding remarks about the inner fault zone structure of the
Baza Fault

The studied fault zone is exceptional because it juxtaposes poorly consolidated sediments in both
fault blocks. The analysis, interpretation, and correlation of the excavated sections (Fig. 67 and
supplementary material), together with the construction of a 3D model (Figs. 70 to 72), have
proven to be useful methods to understand the complex fault zone architecture and to some
extent its evolution. This 4D picture of the fault zone dispels some of the myths about normal
fault zones. Faults are not surfaces but irregular fault zone volumes that are highly variable in 3D
and over short distances (less than 1 m). Fault architecture, deformation styles, and fault facies
are heterogeneous, which in our case is a consequence of the variability in fault geometry, fault
displacement, and mechanical stratigraphy.
Differences in throw and time span of the bounding strands control the distribution of deformation
across the fault zone (E-W direction) and thus the fault zone thickness and symmetry. Along-
strike variations in the geometry of the bounding strands cause redistribution of deformation
in the N-S direction. Southward convergence of the WF and EF leads to increasing interaction
of their fault zones, which is accommodated by secondary subparallel (α) and oblique fault
strands (β and γ). This process increases the throw of the bounding and internal faults and thus
the maturity of the main deformation zone to the south. Where the WF and EF interactions
are greatest, their fault cores merge, giving rise to a single fault zone. Therefore, the evolutionary
stage of the fault zone depends not only on the throw and the time elapsed since the onset of
deformation but also on the geometric variations in the fault system.
The development of the fault zone in poorly lithified, water-saturated, and multi-layered
sediments leads to high heterogeneity in deformation styles (Fig. 78): silty, gravelly, and
limestone lithologies are prone to brittle deformation, while clay-rich lithologies are more likely
to undergo ductile deformation and even fluidization. The mechanical stratigraphy also controls
fault propagation.
The fault facies are heterogeneous. Coarse-grained, high-permeability facies (e.g., subunit 9B)
and clay-rich, low-permeability facies (e.g., subunit 9I) are variable in three dimensions (Fig.
70). Smears along the fault zones are not homogeneous in either their lithology or their spatial
distribution. Clays, silts, sands and gravels are all smeared along the WF and EF. Clay injections
favoured by sub-parallel or oblique (e.g., F50) fault conduits also accommodate fault zone
deformation.
Our results will be helpful in the design of studies of upscaling, subsurface imaging, and reservoir
modelling of highly complex fault zones in poorly lithified sediments. Moreover, the studied
trench site is not the only example of complex inner fault zone structure in the BF. Similar to
the above-described features are observed close to the northern termination of the fault, where
several intrablock faults deform unit 9 (Fig. 80).

4.5. Baza Fault Palaeoseismicity

In this section, we present the first step of the seismogenic characterisation of the BF. It consists
of a palaeoseismological trench study carried out at two sites: Carrizal and Altichuelas. Here,
we present the first palaeoseismological evidence for BF, with a preliminary surface rupturing
history and recurrence intervals of the identified palaeoearthquakes.

Fig. 81. Geological map of the Baza Fault including the location of the 2 palaeoseismological sites excavated
in this study. Lithologies simplified by age.

174 175

<!-- p. 89 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

4.5.1. The Carrizal trench site

The Carrizal site is in the northern sector of the BF, to the north of Baza town (UTM
30S526386/4145217, Fig. 81). The site is the same as that used for the 3D structural analysis of
the fault zone (V-V’ in Fig. 39). The palaeoseismological analysis at the Carrizal site is focused
where F2 intersects recent fluvial deposits. The study area at this site is characterised by a vertical
sequence of different flat landforms (Fig. 82). Erosive incision in this sector developed three
gullies running perpendicular to the BF, which join the Barranco del Agua Creek downstream.
The two recognized flat landforms (surfaces A and B) are related to these gullies. Surface A dips
very gently towards the E and is located ca. 8-10 m above the present thalweg. This older partially
degraded surface is the top of a fluvial terrace, characterised by an alluvial deposit that (formerly)
buried the river valley bottom; this alluvial deposit has a highly variable thickness, controlled by
fault displacement (see below) and valley morphology. Surface B is located approximately 1-3
m above the present thalweg. This surface is the floodplain of the channel. F2 at the Carrizal
site is responsible for an approximately 2-3 m-high fault scarp that separates surface A and the
Pleistocene deposits (unit 11); this fault scarp is not visible in the lower terrace or along the
thalweg of the present drainage network. The Carrizal trench was dug on the left side of Barranco
del Agua Creek, on surface A and across F2 (Fig. 82). The walls of this trench correspond to
sections A00 and A0 (Fig. 83) in the study on the 3D analysis of the fault zone structure. In this
case, we focus on the most recent deposits, instead of on the Plio-Pleistocene sediments.

Fig. 82. Geological–geomorphological map of the Carrizal sector, where the Carrizal trench was excavated. See
figure 81 for location.
Fig. 83. Photomosaics of both walls of the Carrizal trench. They correspond to sections A00 and A0 in the 3D
fault zone study.

The bedrock units exposed in the trench are lacustrine Pleistocene continental deposits belonging
to unit 11 (Fig. 36). Modern topsoil is developed on top of the fluvial deposits as a massive,
poorly sorted, loose, matrix-supported gravel unit, including abundant plant roots (Fig. 84).
Fluvial terrace deposits appear unconformably over the bedrock units. Surface A corresponds
to the top of the fluvial terrace deposits. The terrace units are made of poorly sorted gravels,
sands, silts, and clays. In the north wall of the trench, four terrace units (T1 to T4, Fig. 84)
were distinguished; each of these units consists of poorly sorted, matrix supported, fine gravels
(maximum clast size 1 cm). Sources of gravel clasts are Pleistocene bedrock strata. These fine
gravels are interbedded with fine sands and silt levels up to 3 cm thick. Clay levels \<1 cm thick
are also present. In the south wall (Fig. 84) the terrace consists of the same poorly sorted,
matrix supported fine gravels with thin, fine-grained interbeds. However, in this wall, the
terrace deposits are much thinner and therefore, together with poor outcrop conditions, only
two units are distinguished (TS-lower and TS-upper). Considering the erosive base and the
gentle northward dip of the terrace, it is interpreted that unit TS-upper is equivalent to one of
the uppermost units in the north wall (Fig. 84). T3 is also the youngest dated unit in the wall
(CAR-N8, calibrated age of 22,582–21,993 cal. yr BC). In the south wall of the terrace, the only
dated sample was collected from unit TS-upper (sample CAR-S4), which yielded a calibrated
age of 17,643–17,167 cal. yr BC. Consequently, unit TS-upper is probably younger than unit
T3 and is therefore likely equivalent to unit T4. However, as numerical ages come from detrital
charcoal samples (indicating a maximum age for the containing unit), it cannot be completely

In summary, data from the Carrizal trench indicate a minimum of 5 surface-rupturing events
on the Baza Fault during the last ca. 23,000 yr (our preferred interpretation). An additional
event should be added to this number if we assume that fissure Fiss 4 was the consequence of a
different event. These data also indicate that the 1531 AD Baza Earthquake was not recorded in
the Carrizal trench.

4.5.2.1. Altichuelas trench stratigraphy

The stratigraphic units of the Altichuelas site are grouped into bedrock units, point-bar deposits,
channel deposits, and modern topsoil. To constrain the ages of these units, we collected 8 charcoal
and 11 sand samples (Figs. 87A and 88A). Radiocarbon and OSL analyses were performed on 2
charcoal fragments and 7 sand samples, respectively (Fig. 87, Table 7).

The third scenario assumes that the Baza Earthquake occurred along the Baza Fault, but it did
not produce a surface rupture. In this scenario, the modelled age of the oldest event registered
in the south wall (AltS_e1) overlaps again with two events registered in the north wall: AltN_e1
and AltN_e2 (Fig. 89D). Again, the computed overlaps indicate that AltS_e1 is better correlated
with AltN_e2 (overlap of 0.72) than with AltN_e1 (overlap of 0.08). Therefore, it is postulated
that event AltN_e1 represents the oldest event of the trench (Alt_e1, 16,067–13,775 BC), while
AltS_e1 and AltN_e2 represent the same event, Alt_e2 (13,670–8,815 BC).
The PDF of the next-youngest event recognized in the south wall (AltS_e2) overlaps with events
AltN_e2 and AltN_e3 of the north wall (Fig. 89D). The overlap between AltS_e2 and AltN_e2
is lower than that between AltS_e1 and AltN_e2 (0.72 and 0.08, respectively). Therefore, event
AltN_e2 is included in Alt_e2 together with AltS_e1, as previously discussed. The overlap of
AltS_e2 with event AltS_e3 is lower than the overlap between AltS_e3 and AltN_e3 (0.002
and 0.75, respectively) (Fig. 89C). Consequently, it is proposed that event AltS_e2 represents
palaeoseismic event Alt_e3 (8,657 BC–682 AD). Event Alt_e4 encompasses both AltS_e3 and
AltN_e3, yielding a composite age of 1,125–1,510 AD.

4.5.2.4. Single event displacement in the Altichuelas trench

The top of the basement in the hanging wall of the main fault strand (F05, Figs. 87A and 88A)
indicates a minimum throw for this fault strand of 3.5–0.9 m in the N wall and of 3.2–0.8 m
in the S wall, similar to that discussed above for the Carrizal trench. By dividing this minimum
throw by the number of events postulated above, a minimum single-event displacement of 0.8–
0.1 m is obtained. This minimum single-event displacement is the same for all three scenarios
discussed, as all three involve the same number of events (4).

4.5.3. Earthquake chronologies of the Baza Fault

The above-discussed results are integrated into an earthquake chronology of the BF. This is
carried out by correlating the resulting PDFs for the Carrizal and Altichuelas sites using the
same procedure discussed above. This correlation is based on the quantitative calculation of the
overlap of the event ages obtained from the OxCal modelling described above. In correlating
the site PDFs, it is assumed that the BF is not segmented and is prone to large, generally fault-
wide, characteristic earthquakes. Therefore, the partial surface rupturing histories at the two
sites and the PDF overlap are interpreted as evidence of 8 to 10 earthquakes (EQ1 TO EQ10)
since ca. 45,000 yr BC that ruptured all or most of the length of the BF. This rationale of
using characteristic behaviour is supported by the observation that no segmentation of the fault
has been reported (see section 4.6.1.). At the Carrizal site, 5-6 events are recorded between
45,378 and 558 cal. yr BC (Fig. 84), and 4 events are proposed at the Altichuelas site between
16,239 cal. yr. BC and 1531 AD (Fig. 89). However, because of the geological uncertainties
observed in the trenches, six different scenarios (i.e., earthquake chronologies) are proposed
involving different event ages (Fig. 90). These six alternative chronologies correspond to all the
combinations of the two scenarios discussed for the Carrizal trench (minimum and maximum
number of event scenarios) and the three scenarios of the Altichuelas trench (scenarios 1 to 3)
(Table 8A). An initial visual analysis of the overlapping events for all the proposed chronologies
(Fig. 90A-F) indicates that only the youngest events from the Carrizal trench overlap with the

Earthquake chronology 2

Earthquake chronology 2 encompasses the minimum-number-of events scenario from the
Carrizal trench and Scenario 2 from the Altichuelas trench (Table 8A). The overlap analysis of
the modelled PDFs for the seven older earthquakes (EQ1 to EQ7) of this earthquake chronology
is analogous to that discussed above for earthquake chronology 1 (Table 8B and Fig. 90B).
Differences arise in the younger part of the earthquake chronology. In this case, the youngest
event in the Altichuelas trench (Alt_e4) corresponds to the fault-wide earthquake EQ8 (1,130–

Earthquake produced a surface rupture (not recorded in the trench), an additional earthquake
should be added to the Baza Fault earthquake chronology (EQ9, 1531 AD).

Earthquake chronology 4

Earthquake chronology 4 encompasses the maximum-number-of events scenario from the
Carrizal trench and Scenario 1 from the Altichuelas trench (Table 8A). The overlap analysis
indicates that the four older events from the Carrizal site (Car_e1 to Car_e4) correspond to the
four older fault-wide earthquakes (Table 8B and Fig. 90D): EQ1 (44,260–33,215 BC), EQ 2
(33,450–24,356 BC), EQ 3 (24,225–22,240 BC), and EQ4 (22,220–17,539 BC). When the
rupture histories involved in earthquake chronology 4 (Fig. 90D) are compared, event Car_
e5 partially overlaps with three events at the Altichuelas site (Alt_e1, Alt_e2, and Alt_e3). A
high degree of overlap is found for rupture Car_e5=Alt_e2 (0.41), which is higher than that
for the other ruptures (Car_e5=Alt_e1 overlap 0.28, Car_e5=Alt_e3 overlap 0.24). Moreover,
in earthquake chronology 4, event Car_e6 partially overlaps with events Alt_e1, Alt_e2, and
Alt_e3 (Fig. 90D). The PDF overlap values computed are 0.72 for rupture Car_e6=Alt_e3,
0.04 for rupture Car_e6=Alt_e1, and 0.23 for Care6=Alt_e2. These overlap values indicate that
event Alt_e1 represents earthquake EQ5 (16,182–13,863 BC). The fault-wide earthquake EQ6
encompasses events Car_e5 and Alt_e2 (13,790–8,845 BC). Earthquake EQ7 includes events
Car_e6 and Alt_e3 (8,485–785 BC). Finally, the youngest event in the Altichuelas trench (Alt_
e4, Baza Earthquake) corresponds to the fault-wide earthquake EQ9 (1531AD, Table 8B and
Fig. 90D).

Earthquake chronology 5

Earthquake chronology 5 encompasses the maximum-number-of events scenario from the
Carrizal trench and Scenario 2 from the Altichuelas trench (Table 8A). The overlap analysis
of the seven older earthquakes (EQ1 to EQ7) of earthquake chronology 5 is analogous to that
discussed above for earthquake chronology 4 (Table 8B and Fig. 90E). Differences arise in the
younger part of the earthquake chronology; in this case, the youngest event from the Altichuelas
trench (Alt_e4) corresponds with the fault-wide EQ9 (1130–1505 AD). Furthermore, because
Scenario 2 for the Altichuelas trench assumes that the 1531 Baza Earthquake produced a surface
rupture, an additional earthquake should be added to the Baza Fault earthquake chronology
(EQ10, 1531 AD).

Earthquake chronology 6

Earthquake chronology 6 encompasses the maximum-number-of events scenario from the
Carrizal trench and Scenario 3 from the Altichuelas trench (Table 8A). In this case, the overlap
analysis of the modelled PDFs for the eight older earthquakes (EQ1 to EQ8) of earthquake
chronology 6 is equivalent to that discussed above for earthquake chronology 5 (Table 8B
and Fig. 90F). The only difference is that earthquake chronology 6 only involves 8 fault-wide
earthquakes, as Scenario 3 for the Altichuelas trench assumes that the Baza Earthquake did not
produce a surface rupture. The above-discussed PDF overlap analysis indicates that because of
the uncertainties in our palaeoseismological data, six different earthquake chronologies can be
postulated. These chronologies involve between 8 and 9 earthquakes for the time span of ca.
45,000 BC–1531 AD (earthquake chronologies 1, 2, 4, and 5) or 8 earthquakes for the time
spam of ca. 45,000 BC–1505 AD (earthquake chronologies 3 and 6) (Table 8B).

4.5.4. Earthquake recurrence intervals of the Baza Fault from
palaeoseismological data

To propose a recurrence interval of the Baza Fault, the six surface-rupturing chronologies
discussed above are analysed. In all cases, the ages of the postulated earthquakes are expressed in
terms of PDF (Fig. 90). The methodology proposed by DuRoss et al. (2011) is used to account
for these probability functions in the calculation of the recurrence intervals. Six Monte Carlo
models are performed. Each model is randomly sampled 10,000 times for each earthquake PDF
(previously grouped into 50-year intervals). Each of the 10,000 simulations provides a “random
date” for each earthquake. The intervals between these earthquake random dates are then used
to calculate the elapsed time between pairs of successive earthquakes (e.g., EQ2–EQ1, EQ3–
EQ2, etc.). A PDF for the resulting elapsed times is computed by normalizing the frequency
of each 50-year interval calculated for each pair of intervals. Finally, the PDFs from pairs of
successive earthquakes are combined into a single PDF for each of the six chronologies. These
combined PDFs are considered representative of the earthquake recurrence intervals for the Baza
Fault. All the combined PDFs (Fig. 91) present a broad range of possible recurrence intervals
from 0 to 12,000 yr (95% conﬁdence) with a multimodal distribution. All the earthquake
chronologies present a mode ranging from 4,750 to 5,150 yr (major mode in chronologies 1,
3, 4, and 6). An older minor mode, varying between 9,000 and 9,900 yr, is also recognized.
Finally, a third mode, ranging from 50 to 100 yr, is present in earthquake chronologies 2 and 5.
The 4,750–5,150 yr mode observed in all chronologies (Fig. 91) is a consequence of the wellconstrained elapsed time between earthquake pairs EQ3–EQ4, EQ4–EQ5, and EQ5–EQ6.
Analogously, the 9,000–9,900 yr mode is a consequence of the wide range of elapsed times
between earthquake pairs EQ1-EQ2, EQ2-EQ3, EQ6-EQ7, and EQ7-EQ8. In earthquake
chronologies 2 and 5, the 50–100 yr mode is biased by the calculated time elapsed between the
penultimate earthquake and the 1531 Baza Earthquake (Fig. 91). This is because the PDF is
strongly conditioned by the perfectly constrained occurrence of this last earthquake (its age is not
a PDF, only a point, 1531 AD). Consequently, this youngest mode is partially an artefact related
to data heterogeneity. Moreover, the 50–100 yr mode is also a consequence of the assumption
that the Baza Earthquake produced surface rupture but was not recorded at the Altichuelas site.
This small recurrence interval is completely out of the range of the values within the region, so
this is considered evidence to reject the assumption mentioned above. The two older models can
be explained by assuming a perfect characteristic earthquake model (cf. Shimazaki and Nakata,
1980), i.e., by assuming a constant periodic pattern for the BF. The oldest mode (9,000–9,900 yr,
Fig. 91) seems to represent twice the second-oldest mode (4,750–5,150 yr). Therefore, because
the time elapsed PDFs between all consecutive pairs of earthquakes from EQ3 to EQ6 are
within the range of the second-oldest mode (Fig. 91), the palaeoseismological record of the ﬁrst
group of chronologies could be considered complete between EQ3 and EQ6. The recurrence
interval PDFs before EQ3 and after EQ6 are responsible for the oldest mode (which is twice Fig. 91. Analysis of the Baza Fault EQ recurrence. Each column of graphics shows PDFs for the lapse time
between pairs of consecutive earthquakes (i.e.: EQ2-EQ1, EQ3-EQ2, etc.) for the considered chronologies. Thethe second-oldest mode, as mentioned above). Hence, an event seems to be missing between
bottom row shows the combined PDF of recurrence intervals by adding all the random values from pairs ofEQ1–EQ2, EQ2–EQ3, and EQ6–EQ7. consecutive earthquakes for each chronology, highlighting a multimodal pattern. Axes parameters and scale
are equal for all graphics.

of the BF may be an intermediate value between those obtained from the long-term slip rates 0.15 m in the hanging wall block. Horizontal displacements show a behaviour that globally can be
(2,000-2,200 yr, minimum value) and from the palaeoseismological record (5,300-5,400 yr, described as an overall WSW-ENE extension. Local variations in this overall trend are observed,
maximum value). especially in the hanging wall block, where the direction of the coseismic displacement vectors
is highly influenced by near-field irregularities related to irregularities in the fault geometry.
In the northern sector of the BF, the horizontal displacement direction varies from NW-SE
(approximately N150E in the Cortes de Baza town) to WSW-ENE (approximately N085E in
Baza town) (Fig. 93). In the southern sector, the horizontal displacement direction varies from
WSW-ENE (approximately N085E in Baza town) to SW-NE (approximately N040E close to
Caniles town) (Fig. 93).

Fig. 92. Probability Distribution Function (PDF) and Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF) for the Baza Fault
according to the adjusted Weibull distribution, using the computed recurrence times from the palaeoseismological
record. The dashed line indicates the elapsed time since the 1531 Baza Earthquake (490 yr in 2021).

4.6.4. Rupture geodetic scenario for the MMAX event

Once the Mmax magnitude earthquake has been estimated, we model a geodetic scenario for this
event. From the BF geometry (total length, dip and seismogenic depth), we model the Mmax
magnitude using a uniform displacement among blocks of 0.65 m, which involves a seismic
moment on the order of 1.24 ·1026 dyn·cm, i.e., a Mw 6.7 event (sensu Wells and Coppersmith,
1994).
The obtained results are shown in Fig. 93. Vertical and horizontal deformations can be observed
jointly, showing the large area affected from a geodetic point of view for such earthquakes.
Fig. 93. Geodetic scenario for the Mmax magnitude earthquake (Mw 6.7) showing both vertical (colouredDownward permanent displacements in the hanging wall block reach values that exceed 0.40
contours) and horizontal (black arrows) permanent deformations. This figure shows a modelled fault trace, not
m, embracing the entire central half of this block. In contrast, upward permanent displacements the real one. Input parameters are presented.
in the footwall block are on the order of a few centimetres. Two areas of maximum vertical

208 209

<!-- p. 106 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

These results show the typical behaviour of a normal fault (e.g., Sun et al., 2008; Kobayashi et
al., 2012) and fit geological kinematic data (Fig. 56). This type of displacement map could help
in the design of geodetic networks focused on the study of regional or local deformations in
the area. For example, observed local anomalies of the displacement vectors close to the fault,
diverging from the overall direction of the regional deformation, indicate that GPS stations
should not be installed too close to the fault traces to avoid these local effects.

4.6.5. Concluding remarks about the Baza Fault seismic potential

A seismogenic characterisation of the BF is carried out based on fault data (geometry, kinematics,
slip rates, and seismic data) to discuss fault segmentation and to evaluate the seismic potential
of the fault. The BF constitutes a single seismogenic master fault according to geometric
and kinematic segmentation criteria (Table 9). The seismic potential of the BF is defined by
calculating Mmax and recurrence times using different empirical relations (Table 10).
The expected Mmax considering an entire rupture of the BF ranges has been estimated to be Mw
6.6±0.4 (using Wells and Coppersmith, 1994), Mw 6.7±0.4 (using Leonard, 2010), Mw 6.9
(using Wesnousky, 2008) and Mw 7.1 (using Stirling et al., 2002).
Recurrence times between Mmax events range between approximately 2,000 and 2,200 yr
(CV=1.45). Furthermore, the recurrence times calculated from the palaeoseismological record,
using both the BPT and the Weibull distributions, are approximately 5,300–5,400 yr (CV=0.46).
We suggest that the actual recurrence times for the BF may range between the overestimated
value obtained from the long-term slip rates (2,000–2,200 yr) and the underestimated value
obtained from the palaeoseismological record (5,300–5,400 yr).
A geodetic rupture scenario has been modelled for a Mmax event of Mw 6.7 (Fig. 93). Vertical
permanent displacements are higher in the hanging wall block, reaching values that exceed 0.40
m in the central part of this block. Horizontal permanent displacements present values that
exceed 0.20 m and 0.15 m in the footwall and hanging wall blocks, respectively. This scenario
shows overall WSW–ENE extension, which fits with the present geodynamic setting of the
Central Betic Cordillera. However, local variations in the direction of extension are observed due
to the variations in the fault geometry, especially in the hanging wall block.
The calculated seismogenic parameters of the BF will be relevant for future seismic hazard studies
in the Central Betic Cordillera, considered one of the most seismically active areas in the Iberian
Peninsula.

CHAPTER 5

THE GALERA FAULT

<!-- p. 108 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

\5. The Galera Fault

In this section we characterise the other major structure within the Baza Subbasin: the active GF.
In the first step, we describe the stratigraphy related to the fault and define the offset materials.
We define the surface geometry of the fault array (length, dip, strike, and width) and fault
kinematics. In addition, we discuss the along-strike geometric variations of the fault. We also
provide new GPS geodetic data, which allow us to define the short-term slip rate of this fault.
Moreover, we present the first palaeoseismological data for the GF. These data comprise a
description of deformed Quaternary deposits and an interpretation in terms of palaeoevents.
This palaeoseismological study is not concluded at present. The final results will consist of a
complete surface rupturing history and the calculation of recurrence intervals for the identified
palaeoearthquakes. We are still waiting for the results of several OSL analyses that will permit
us to constrain the age of the most recent deformed horizons of the trenches. Consequently, the
characterisation of the seismogenic potential of the GF will be carried out in future works.
To conclude, the characterisation of the GF, we evaluate the tectonic control that this structure
exerts on the Baza Subbasin landscape. For this, we estimate the geomorphological impact of the
GF on the topography and on the drainage network in the study area.

5.1. Stratigraphy of the Galera region

The GF extends through the northeastern sector of the Baza Subbasin, offsetting the Pliocene
to Pleistocene deposits of the Baza Palaeolake. According to Torres et al. (2003) and Gibert
et al. (2007a), the lacustrine deposits of the Baza Subbasin are distributed in three concentric
lithologic zones corresponding to different sedimentary environments (Fig. 16). In this study, a
series of informal lithostratigraphic units are defined for the Galera region (Figs. 94 and 95), for
use as stratigraphic markers to estimate the vertical displacement produced by the GF. The age
of these units is based on previous biostratigraphic and magnetostratigraphic studies (e.g., Vera
et al., 1985; Martín-Suárez, 1988; Agustí and Martín-Suárez, 1994; Garcés et al., 1997; Oms et
al., 2000 and 2011; Gibert et al., 2007a and 2007b). Some of the units are the same defined for
the Baza region, so we use the same numbers as in section 4.1. For the non-correlatable units,
we use codes composed of G (Galera) and a number (e.g., unit G2).

<!-- p. 109 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

1.6 to 1.1 Ma, Martín-Suárez, 1988; Alberdi et al., 1989; Agustí and Martín-Suárez, 1994;

216 217

<!-- p. 110 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

Garcés et al., 1997) alluvio-palustrine (conglomerates, carbonate silts and gypsum, unit G7,
Fig. 96G) and palustrine-lacustrine (carbonate silts and limestones, unit G8, Fig. 96H) deposits
(Lupiani-Moreno et al., 1994). Conglomerates within unit G7 present abundant metamorphic
clasts from the Betic Internal Zones basement. Unit G7 appears mainly in the NW fault block,
although some outcrops can be found locally on top of the SE block (Figs. 94 and 95). However,
palustrine-lacustrine unit G8 crops out exclusively in the NW block of the GF (Figs. 94 and 95),
suggesting the existence of local lacustrine depocentres to the NW of the GF during the end of
the endorheic stage of the Baza Subbasin.
The above-described units change laterally in the study area. To the W, units G2 and G3 change
laterally into unit G2b (Figs. 94, 95, and 97). This unit consists of a lower member of greenish
silts with abundant interstitial gypsum (Fig. 96I and J) and an upper member of white massive
gypsum (Fig. 96I), interpreted as mudflat and shallow lacustrine facies, respectively (Gibert et al.,
2007a). To the N, to the NW and to the NE (to the subbasin margin), the gypsum and carbonate
contents decrease, and the succession becomes rich in sandy silts, sands (unit 8, see section 4.1.),
and conglomerates (unit 6b (see section 4.1.), close to the N and NW margins (Figs. 94 and
95). That is, the fluvio-palustrine facies change into more siliciclastic marginal alluvial fan facies.
To the SW, (Figs. 94 and 95), units G2 to G8 change gradually into an alternanating laminated
silts, carbonate silts and clays (unit 11, section 4.1.) (intermediate sedimentary zone of Gibert
et al., 2007a) that become increasingly gypsipherous in the central part of the Baza Subbasin
(unit 12, section 4.1.) (inner sedimentary zone sensu Gibert et al., 2007a) (Figs. 94 and 95). It
is not possible to distinguish between different lithostratigraphic units either in the intermediate
or the inner zones due to the homogeneous sedimentary succession. We identify several seismite
levels in the sedimentary succession in the study area. These structures are present mainly in the
Pleistocene, marginal lacustrine sediments (units G4 to G6) (Fig. 18) and in the marginal sandy
deposits to the NE of the study area (unit 8) (Fig. 18).
Exorheic sedimentation is represented in the GF area by Pleistocene-Holocene sediments, such
as fluvial terraces, floodplain deposits and recent colluvial slope deposits (units G9, Fig. 96K
and L).

5.2. Galera Fault geometry

In the following sections, we describe the GF surface geometry. We define the structure of the
fault zone and the damage zone, defining fault sectors based on their surface geometry. We also
discuss along-strike geometric variations of the GF, in this case in terms of fault deep geometry
and thickness of the sedimentary cover.

5.2.1. Surface geometry of the Galera Fault

The general surface geometry of the GF (Fig. 98) consists of an approximately 30 km-long fault
zone striking SW-NE and dipping steeply to the NW or vertically (Fig. 99). We differentiate two
main fault blocks: a SE block and a NW block. The GF is a strike-slip fault with a minor vertical
component responsible for the uplift of the SE block (see section 5.3).

Fig. 98. Surface geometry map of the Galera Fault. Dark red blobs represent towns.

Fig. 99. Geological cross sections across the Galera Fault. Locations are shown in Figure 94.

222 223

<!-- p. 113 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

The fault array is formed by a set of closely spaced fault strands. The orientation, arrangement,
and number of these fault strands are variable along strike and influence the width and geometry
of the entire fault system (Fig. 99). We divide the GF into four sectors from NE to SW based on
the surface geometry of the fault array (Fig. 98).
Sector 1 corresponds with the most northeastern portion of the GF. It extends 5.5 km (Fig. 98),
strikes roughly SW-NE, and has a N45E orientation in the SW and a N70E orientation in the
NE. At the map scale, the fault zone is formed by a single major fault strand that dips 70° to
the NW (section I-I’ in Fig. 99 and Fig. 100A) to vertically (Fig. 100B). In sector 1 the fault
zone presents a linear pattern (Fig. 98). In the NE termination of the GF, some secondary faults
branch out to the N and NE from the major fault (Figs. 98 and 101). Far to the E, the GF does
not present a surface expression and is covered by recent quaternary deposits. To the SW, sector
1 dies out and is replaced by sector 2, which is situated approximately 3.5 km to the SW.

Fig. 101. Secondary fault strands offsetting recent alluvial fan deposits in the NE termination of the Galera
Fault.

Sector 2 (Fig. 98) is the continuation of sector 1 to the SW. The connection between sectors 1
and 2 is achieved by means of a 3.5 km-long, SSW-NNE (N15E)-striking fault that conforms
to a kilometric left fault bend (Figs. 98 and 102). This SSW-NNE fault dips 45 to 50° to the
WNW (section II-II’ in Figs. 99 and 103), although it steepens to the SW. It produces an up to
50 m-high scarp and a 2 km-wide, rhombic-shaped fluvial valley in its hanging wall (Figs. 102
and 104).

Fig. 100. Galera Fault outcrops along sector 1. A) The main fault strand of the fault in sector 1. B) The Galera
Fault close to its NE termination. Fig. 102. A) Inset marking the location of B). B) Elevation map displaying the left fault bend between sectors 1
and 2, and the rhombic-shaped valley developed in its hanging wall. Locations of pictures in figures 103 and
104 are indicated. The white dotted line highlights the widening of the Barbata River valley associated to the
lef fault bend of the Galera Fault.

224 225

<!-- p. 114 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

Fig. 103. A) and B) Pictures of the SSW-NNE-striking fault conforming the left fault bend between sectors 1 and
\2. Picture locations in figure 102. Red line indicates the fault in section (solid line) and in map view (dashed
line).

In sector 2, which is 9 km long (Fig. 98), the GF is oriented SW-NE, and deformation is
distributed along a damage zone of variable width (between 500 and 1,500 m). The fault array
consists of a SW-NE-striking fault zone from which a set of N-S fault strands branch out and
extend N for several hundred metres (Fig. 104).
The SW-NE fault zone presents a segmented array consisting of a series of fault strands forming
a right-stepping en échelon pattern. The fault strands strike N40E to N60E (Fig. 105) and dip
steeply (between 65° and 80° to the NW) or vertically (sections III-III’ and IV-IV’ in Fig. 99
and Figs. 106 and 107). The right stepovers are defined by the overlapping pairs of these faults,
with separations between them ranging from 100 to 300 m. The N-S-oriented faults (Fig. 105)
are short fractures striking from N170E to N10E and dipping ca. 60°. Most of them dip to the
W or are nearly vertical, although E-dipping faults occur locally (Fig. 108). The length of these
faults increases westwards, from 1,500 to 600 m (Fig. 105). These structures bound narrow N-Soriented blocks that are tilted or rotated 5 to 15° to the NE (section V-V’ in Fig. 99).
Fig. 104. A) Panoramic view of the fluvial valley developed in the Galera Fault hanging wall associated to the
left fault bend. B)Northward tilting in the NW block of the Galera Fault. Red line is the fault in section (solid
line) and in map view (dashed line).

<!-- p. 115 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

Fig. 105. Close-up of the fault array in sector 2. Locations of Figures 106 to 109 are indicated.

Fig. 107. A) Panoramic view and B) Outcrop view of one of the main SW-NE fault strand of the Galera Fault
in sector 2. Location in Figure 105. Red line is the fault in section (solid line) and in map view (dashed line).
Offset lithostratigraphic units are also represented in A.

Apart from these block rotations, deformation along sector 2 produces an overall tilt (10°)
towards the N and NW in the NW block of the GF (Fig. 105 and sections III-III’ and IV-IV’ in
Fig. 99). This tilting is more accentuated (ca. 20 to 45°) close to the left fault bend, towards the
fluvial valley developed in the fault hanging wall (Fig. 104). To the E and SE, in the SE block of
the GF, deformation is accommodated by means of a kilometric-scale gentle SW-NE syncline
subparallel to the SW-NE fault zone (Fig. 105) and hectometric-scale folds that strike obliquely
(N15E) to sub-perpendicularly (N120E to N160E) with respect to the SW-NE fault zone (Figs.
105 and 109).

Figure 105.

In sector 3, which is 7.5 km long (Figs. 98 and 110), the GF is oriented SW-NE and dips
ca. 70° to the NW. Deformation is distributed along band formed two roughly linear major
fault strands (striking N60E to N70E) (Figs. 110 and 111). Between these two linear faults,
ca. N35E oblique faults develop (Fig. 112), leading to an anastomosing damage zone (Fig.
110). The two major faults bound a damage zone that gradually narrows towards the SW from
550 m to 200 m. Minor oversteps (50 to 150 m) and fault bends are identified along their
traces. These major bounding faults, together with the N30E fractures, individualize elongated

Fig. 110. Close-up of the fault array in sector 3. Locations of Figures 111 and 112 are indicated.

Sector 4 is contiguous to sector 3 and corresponds with the western surface termination of the
GF (Figs. 98 and 113). This sector is 9 km long, strikes SW-NE, and dips 70° NW to vertical
(Figs. 114 and 115). The exposure conditions are very poor in this sector, so fault traces can
barely be observed. The fault array apparently consists of separate ca. N45E fault strands that
link with a pair of ca. N25E fractures, leading to a right-stepping en echelon pattern (Fig.
113). The right stepovers between the N25E faults show separations of approximately 1000 m.
Some secondary fractures subparallel to or branching out from the major faults are observed.
In sector 4, hectometric-scale drag and rollover folds are present in the NW block (Fig. 113
and section VII-VII’ in Fig. 99). To the SW, the GF seems to die out, as it does not present a
surface expression.

5.2.3. Concluding remarks about the Galera Fault geometry

We define this fault as a 30 km-long structure, striking SW-NE, and highly dipping NW to
vertical. The fault presents an uplifted SE block and a downthrown NW block. This fault offsets
lower Pliocene to Middle Pleistocene rocks and Holocene fluvial terraces (Fig 94). The most
recent endorheic deposits (units G7 and G8) crop out mainly in the downthrown NW block
(Figs. 94 and 96), which indicates sedimentary control of the GF during the endorheic stage of
the Baza Subbasin. The GF can be divided into four sectors based on the surface geometry of the
fault array (Fig. 98): i) in sector 1, the fault zone consists of a single strand and corresponds to
the NE termination; ii) in sector 2, the fault zone is formed by WSW-ENE faults arranged in a
segmented en echelon pattern and by minor N-S faults (Fig. 105); iii) in sector 3, the fault zone
is wider and presents a more complex anastomosing pattern (Fig. 110); and iv) in sector 4, the
fault zone is characterised by a highly segmented en echelon pattern and corresponds to the SW
fault termination (Fig. 113). Between sectors 1 and 2, approximately N-S-oriented fault strands
accommodate deformation produced by a left restraining fault bend, which is responsible for the
development of a pull-apart basin (Fig. 102).
We interpret the along-strike geometric variations between the four sectors of the GF as result
of i) changes in the orientation of the basement fault (Figs. 117 and 118) and ii) a southward
increase in the thickness of the sedimentary cover over the basement fault (Fig. 119).

5.3. Galera Fault kinematics

In this section, we discuss the GF kinematics. We analyse the direction and sense of displacement
together with fault slip rates. The direction and sense of displacement are determined by the
analysis of kinematic indicators (Fig. 120) and the surface fault geometry. Slip rates are estimated
from geodetic data and offset stratigraphic markers.

5.3.1. Direction and sense of slip of the Galera Fault

Fig. 119. A) Sketches illustrating how the thickness of the sedimentary overburden of the basement Galera

The geometry of R shears and the complexity of damage zones could be also been controlled by
other parameters. For example, in oblique slip faulting contexts, in the overburden of a basement
fault with no strike changes, the developed fault patterns will be more en echelon (more oblique
to the buried fault) with an increasing strike-slip component in the basement fault (Richard et
al., 1995). We discard the ratio between slip components as the conditioning parameter of the
geometric variations in the GF, as the fault presents a dominant strike-slip component in all the
sectors (see below, section 5.3.1.).

The presence of mesoscale kinematic indicators on some of the slickensides of the outcropping
SW-NE faults evidence the lateral slip of the GF (Figs. 120). These indicators are not very
abundant as the slickensides are not well preserved in many outcrops due to the high erodibility
of the juxtaposed deposits. Indicators consist mainly of slickenlines and gypsum slickenfibres
(Fig. 121). The mean trend of these lineations range from ca. N240 to ca. N205 (Fig. 120),
showing horizontal to oblique plunges (mean rake of 25°, ranging between 0 to ca. 50°, Figs.

oriented R shears of the GF have a dip-slip component.

The lateral slip of the GF can also be inferred from its surface geometry, as we discussed above.
We interpret the different structural arrangements in the four sectors of the fault as a result
of strike-slip kinematics (Riedel, 1929; Tchalenko, 1970). Both subsidiary Riedel (R1) shears

242 243

<!-- p. 123 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

(striking N25E to N45E) and rotated R2 shears (N45E to N70E) conform to the main fault
zone. The strike-slip component of the GF is also responsible for the 2 km-wide, left releasing
fault bend developed between sectors 1 and 2 (Fig. 102), which we interpret as a pull-apart basin
(sensu Mann et al., 1983). According to this interpretation, the N15E fault linking sectors 1 and
2 acts as the eastern sidewall fault of the basin. Its geomorphic expression, the vertical offset of
the stratigraphic markers (section II-II’ in Fig. 99), and the presence of slickenlines with rakes
of ca. 90° (Fig. 120), reflect a pure dip-slip component of displacement, indicating that it is a
normal fault. The western border of the pull-apart basin is not defined by a western sidewall fault
but by a gentle geomorphic scarp and a gentle monocline towards the SE (Fig. 102), displaying
a half-graben geometry. We interpret the fault strands striking N170E to N15E in sector 2 as
structures accommodating the extension related to the left fault bend between sectors 1 and 2
(section V-V’ in Fig. 99), as they are subparallel to it and show both horizontal and dip-slip
slickenlines (ranging between 3 to 80°, Fig. 120).
The sense of displacement of the GF can be inferred from its surface geometry. The rightstepping en echelon pattern of the R shears within the fault zone (Figs. 105, 110, and 113) is in
agreement with a left-lateral sense of displacement along the basement strike-slip GF (Riedel,
\1929) (Fig. 118). We hypothesised that the basement fault responsible for generating the surface
array in of the GF could be the ancient right-lateral Cadiz-Alicante Fault (Sanz de Galdeano,
1983 and 2008); hence we interpret the GF as a left-lateral reactivated portion of this structure.

5.3.2.2. Short-term slip rates: geodetic data

In this section, we discuss the GPS-derived results obtained for the GF. As we stated for the BF,
all of the sites located in the Baza Subbasin move towards the WSW at different rates with respect
to stable Eurasia (Fig. 61), i.e., roughly perpendicular to the convergence direction between the
Nubian and Eurasian plates (N323E ± 1.8; Argus et al., 2011). We established two GPS sites
related to the GF: i) site 5700, located in the NW block of the GF, yielding a residual velocity
(E component) of -1.2 ± 0.5 mm/yr (Fig. 123 and Table 11); and ii) site 5600, located in the
SE block of the GF, yielding a residual velocity (E component) of -0.7 ± 0.6 mm/yr (Fig. 123
and Table 11).
To analyse the slip rate of the strike-slip GF we also used the N065E (fault direction) projected
component of motion of the GPS-derived velocities (Fig. 123 and Table 11). In this case, we
subtract the residual velocities of the stations located to the north (site 5700, set 1) and to
the south (site 5600, set 2) of the GF (Fig. 123). After this analysis, we obtain a value for the
N065E component of motion of VN065E = 0.5 ± 0.3 mm/yr (Fig. 123 and Table 11). This
value indicates that the GF features a sinistral strike-slip component. Once again, these are the
first quantitative strike-slip rates attributed to the GF, as previous geological studies quantified
only the vertical component of the fault. In any case, the geological vertical slip rates (ranging
between 0.1 and 0.2 mm/yr; García-Tortosa et al., 2008b and 2011a; Sanz de Galdeano et
al., 2012) are lower than our GPS-derived strike-slip rates. Consequently, our data further
support the interpretation of the GF as a strike-slip fault with a minor dip-slip component. Our
GPS-derived residual velocities are not accurate enough to analyse the perpendicular-to-strike
component of movement (transpression or transtension) of the GF.

given in mm/yr. B) Short-term slip rates for the Galera Fault.

5.3.3. Concluding remarks about the Galera Fault kinematics

The slip direction and slip sense of the GF are inferred from the analysis of kinematic indicators
(Figs. 120 and 121), the geometric patterns of the fault array, and offset stratigraphic markers
(Fig. 122). This analysis corroborates that the GF presents a main left-lateral displacement
component and a minor vertical displacement.
Long-term vertical slip rates are calculated from the stratigraphic markers, ranging between 0.02
and 0.05 mm/year. The maximum vertical offset and, hence, the maximum vertical slip rate
occur in sector 3. Short-term horizontal slip rates, i.e., for the main strike-slip component, are
quantified for the first time for the GF, resulting in 0.5 ± 0.3 mm/yr (Table 11).

5.4. Tectonic geomorphology: morphometric analysis

In this section we evaluate the tectonic control of the GF over the Quaternary landscape of the
Baza Subbasin, focusing on the geomorphological impact that fault displacement has had on the
topography and drainage network in the study area.

5.4.1. Topographic analysis

We analyse the topography of the study area from the elevation (Fig. 124) and slope angle maps
(Fig. 125) and from the profile relief ratio (PRR, sensu Duval and Tucker, 2015).
The elevation map (Fig. 124) shows that the highest areas (other than the basin margins)
correspond to the preserved glacis surface, which also presents the lowest slope angle values
(Fig. 125). In contrast, the lowest areas extend to the SW of the study area, where the fluvial
drainage system is more downcut into the Baza Subbasin sedimentary infill. However, the
most remarkable feature of the elevation map is that it reveals a sharp asymmetry between the Fig. 124. Elevation map of the Galera sector extracted from high-resolution DEM (5 m per pixel). Fault traces
southern and northern sides of the main river valleys, i.e., those of the Guardal (downstream of and main river streams are depicted.
the confluence with the Galera River), Galera and Barbata Rivers (Fig. 124). The relief on the
southern side is generally higher than the relief on the northern side. Despite the higher relief of We also observe asymmetries in the patterns of ridges and fluvial valleys in both the elevation
the SE block of the fault, the GF does not present a defined mountain front, as it appears highly and slope angle maps, especially along sectors 2 and 3 of the GF. South of the GF, the ridges
eroded by the drainage network and has receded more than 2 km in some sectors (Fig. 124). are sharp, and the valleys are narrow with steep slopes (30-45°). North of the fault, the ridges
are generally smoother, and the valleys are wider with gentler slopes (10-20°) (Fig. 125). These
observations also highlight local topographic features associated with the GF, such as the
rhombic-shaped valley related to the pull-apart basin between sectors 1 and 2 (Fig. 102) and the
local relief generated by uplift along the GF strands in sector 4 (Fig. 113).
