128). The displacement observed in sector 2 is mostly related to folding in both fault blocks, as
the modelled glacis surface describes a gentle anticline and a pair of synclines (P3 and P4, Fig.
\128) (García-Tortosa et al., 2011a). In sector 3, we estimate the glacis offset between 85±25 and
90±30 m (P5 and P6, Fig. 128). In this case, the degree of uncertainty is higher as the erosion
is more accentuated to the SW. In sector 3, the deformation is related mostly to the fault offset
but also to the gentle tilting towards the S.
Knowing these approximate vertical glacis displacements and considering a minimum age for the
capture of the basin ranging between 500 and 600 ka (Gibert et al., 2007b), we estimate the total
tectonic uplift rates for each sector. We obtain a minimum uplift rate in sector 1 (approximately
0.03 mm/yr) and maximum in sector 3 (0.18± 0.04 mm/yr). The obtained values are slightly
different to those obtained by García-Tortosa et al. (2007 and 2011a), which range between
0.08 and 0.24 mm/year in sector 2 (equivalent to our profile P4, Fig. 128). These differences
derive from the different age assigned by these authors to the glacis surface (a minimum of 205
ka and a maximum of 600 ka). Moreover, the uplift rates we obtained using the glacis surface
as marker are higher than the vertical fault slip rates we obtain using stratigraphic markers (0.02
to 0.05 mm/yr). This difference could derive from the age uncertainties of the offset markers
(especially of the stratigraphic marker). Differences could be also related to the fact that we
calculate the fault slip rate considering only the fault throw (Fig. 122), but we calculate the fault
uplift considering both the fault throw and the folding (Fig. 128).

5.4.3 Drainage network analysis

We carry out an analysis of the drainage network pattern and calculate a series of geomorphic
indices for the catchments and streams related to the GF (Table 12). We extract longitudinal
stream profiles, and calculate the normalized channel steepness index (ksn), basin asymmetry

which the PRR is calculated for the four fault sectors and for the left fault bend bounding the pull-apart basin
between sectors 1 and 2. Obtained PRR values are close to 1, indicating that near and farther profiles present The study of the drainage network pattern reveals that the geometry of the main rivers in the
very similar reliefs in all sectors. study area is related to the GF traces (Fig. 129). The confluence between the Barbata and Orce
Rivers coincides with the pull-apart basin sector. Moreover, at this point, the main stream
draining the study area (Galera-Guardal River) turns to the WSW and flows subparallel to the
fault zone along sectors 2 and 3. However, in sector 4, the Guardal River crosses through the
fault zone, decoupling from the GF.5.4.2. Analysis of the glacis surface

The modelled glacis surface reveals that the vertical dip-slip component of the GF is responsible
for the displacement of the glacis (Fig. 128). The performed analysis allows us to estimate the
vertical offset of the glacis and the vertical uplift rate of the GF along sectors 1 to 3. However,
the used method (García-Tortosa et al., 2008b and 2011a) is not applicable in sector 4 because
of the high uncertainty derived from the long distance between the preserved glacis surfaces and
the high volume of eroded material in the central part of the subbasin (where the GF is).
Therefore, we estimate the vertical offset of the glacis in 20±5 m in sector 1 (P1 in Fig. 128).

Moreover, the drainage network map shows asymmetry between the catchments located in the
NW and SE fault blocks (Fig. 129). Catchments located mostly in the SE fault block, in the
southern side of the GF, present short main streams. In addition, they present a radial pattern
in sectors 2 and 3, where streams initiate from the glacis surface and diverge in all directions.
In contrast, catchments located entirely in the NW fault block present, in general, longer main
streams. Moreover, they initiate in the northern subbasin margin. In addition, some channels
branch out upstream from the fault zone, especially from fault-strike-parallel strands, leading to
catchment widening in the SE block of the GF (Fig. 126A).

The asymmetry factor (AF) was calculated for all the catchments within the fault zone of the
GF and its vicinity (Fig. 134A). Values obtained for catchments located in the fault zones from
sector 1 to the eastern part of sector 2 show an overall asymmetry to the NW. Moreover, the
AF of catchments that drain across the fault zone in the western part of sector 2 and sector 3
indicate, in general, a marked asymmetry to the N and NNE. Some catchments located to the
E that drain into the Orce River show asymmetry to the E. Conversely, catchments to the N of
the Galera River that are situated in the NW block of the GF are generally asymmetric to the
SE. Finally, values from those catchments in the western part of the study area reflect an overall
asymmetry to the SW. The main Barbata River also has a marked asymmetry downstream,
defined by a right deflection close to its confluence with the Orce River (Fig. 134B).

We obtained the valley floor width-to-height ratio (Vf) for 8 stream channels (St1, St4 to St8,
St10, and St12, Fig. 135A) to quantify the possible effects of the GF on valley incision. As an
overview, Vf values are much higher along the streams located entirely in the NW fault block
(St5, St7, and St10), which are associated with wide, U-shaped valleys dominated by erosional
processes. In contrast, cross-fault streams show variable Vf values (St1, St4, St6, St8, and St12).
When they flow through the SE fault block (upthrown block of the GF) and across the fault
zone, they present low Vf values (\<1), i.e., V-shaped valleys. As soon as these streams reach the
NW block (downthrown of the GF), they show higher Vf values (>1), i.e., wider valleys. In
sector 1, stream St1 also shows this variation between fault blocks, but the Vf values are always
higher than 1.

5.4.4. Interpretation of geomorphic results: geomorphic evidence of
Galera Fault activity

The landscape of the Baza Subbasin consists of very young relief formed after Middle Pleistocene
basin capture (Fig. 14) (Calvache and Viseras, 1995; Díaz-Hernández and Juliá, 2006; Scott and
Gibert, 2009; García-Tortosa et al., 2008a, 2008b, and 2011a; Moral and Balanyá, 2020). Since
this event, erosion has developed an impressive badlands landscape. Despite the low slip rate
of the GF, the activity of this active structure has controlled the development of this landscape,
leaving an imprint on both the topography and the drainage network. In this section, we attempt
to analyse and understand the geomorphic evidence of this tectonic control using our data about
the fault geometry, kinematics, and landscape morphometric analysis.
Although the GF presents a main left-lateral component of displacement, the slow horizontal
motion has left a subtle imprint over the landscape in the study area. These effects can be observed
through a series of geomorphic evidence. Evidence of horizontal fault displacement is deduced
from the morphology of cross-fault ridges and valleys. In some cross-fault streams, we observe
likely left deflections and channels that are subparallel to fault strike (Fig. 126A). These are
geomorphological indicators that can be used for determining the fault slip sense (e.g., Allen et
al., 1984; Li and Yang, 1998), supporting in the left-lateral slip deduced from the analysis of the
fault geometry and kinematic indicators (Figs. 118 and 120). According to Zhang et al. (2003),
channel deflections also involve asymmetrical valley widening downstream of the strike-slip
fault. However, the transverse profiles we trace along some of the cross-fault valleys (Fig. 135A)
do not show any evidence of this phenomenon. The explanation of this might lie in the fact that
streams are relatively small and ephemeral, with no accentuated lateral migration.
The branching out of some streams upstream from the fault (Fig. 126A) reflects the growth
of new sub-catchments that initiate from the lengthened channels that are subparallel to the
fault strike. These new sub-catchments expanded laterally, increasing the basin area upstream
from the fault and leading to the shrinkage of neighboring basins (Fig. 126A). Numerical
modelling of near-fault landform evolution under strike-slip fault motion conditions shows that
there is a strong relation between fault slip rates and landscape evolution along strike-slip fault

5.4.5. Concluding remarks about the geomorphic impact of the Galera
Fault

This study provides a good example for understanding how a slow left-lateral fault controls
the development of a recent landscape. The Middle Pleistocene endorheic-exorheic transition
(ca. 500 ka) in the study area provides a good marker (glacis surface) to constrain the tectonogeomorphological evolution of the low-rate GF.
The slow fault slip rate and the high erodibility of the late Quaternary rocks on both sides of the
GF allow a rapid landscape response to the instability generated by GF activity. This is reflected
by the lateral expansion of catchments upstream from the fault, ridges extending across the fault
(Fig. 126), and the absence of fault-facing facets (PRR values close to 1, Fig. 127).
The effect that the horizontal left-lateral GF slip has over the drainage network geometry and
the topography can be observed from the morphology of cross-fault valleys, leading to leftward
stream deflections, channels that are subparallel to fault strike, and the formation of a pull-apart
basin.
The vertical component of displacement of the GF, with the uplift of the SE fault block with
respect to the NW block, has had a strong influence on the topography and has deformed
the Middle Pleistocene glacis surface. The glacis displacement ranges between 20 ± 5 and 90
± 30 m and is higher along the central part of the fault (sectors 2 and 3) and lower at the NE
termination (sector 1) (Fig. 128). Tectonic uplift causes catchment asymmetries to the NE in
the SE block and to the SE in the NW block, as deduced from the AF index (Fig. 131A). It also
generates drainage network asymmetries between the SE and NW fault blocks. In the SE fault
block, streams are shorter as they initiate from the uplifted fault area (Figs. 129, 131, and 133),
thus presenting steeper longitudinal profiles (Fig. 133), higher valley incision (low Vf values)
(Fig. 135) and smaller catchments. Uplift is more intense in the central part (sectors 3 and 2),
as suggested by the radial drainage pattern (Fig. 129), the steeper longitudinal profiles, and the
high valley slope angles (Fig. 125). These effects induced by the fault uplift are responsible for the
development of a spectacular badland landscape in the SE block related to the intense recession
of the uplifted SE block (Figs. 136 and 137). In contrast, in the NW fault block, streams initiate
from the basin margin and are generally longerand have larger catchments. Hence, they present
gentler longitudinal profiles with less intense valley incision (high Vf values, Fig. 135).
In summary, this qualitative and quantitative geomorphic analysis, based on the application of
several geomorphic indices, and its integration with structural field data of the GF enable us to
demonstrate that slow strike-slip faults also exert control on the landscape in semiarid conditions
and to reconstruct the recent evolution (last ca. 500 ka) of the relief in response to active faulting.

Fig. 136. A) Schematic sketch and section illustrating the influence of the Galera Fault on the recent evolution
of the landscape in the Galera sector. The intense erosion of the highly erodible lacustrine rocks, together with
the tectonic control of the slow oblique Galera Fault, have modelled the current landscape. The landscape is
characterized by drainage network asymmetries between the two fault blocks (differences in valley incision,
catchment extension, and stream geometry), and the badlands landscape in the southern block related to
the receded mountain front. B) Oblique view (Google Earth) showing the badland landscape developed S of
the Galera River favored by the Galera Fault uplift. View from W to E. Location of Fig. 137A and B is indicated.

5.5. Galera Fault palaeoseismicity

Although several outstanding outcrops of Pliocene and Pleistocene seismites are located in the
Galera region (Alfaro et al., 2010), there are no previous studies on the palaeoseismological
record of the GF. In this section, we present the first steps of the palaeoseismological study we
carried out in the GF and the palaeoearthquake evidence found in excavated trenches. Currently,
we cannot properly constrain the age of the identified events, as we still do not have the results of
the analyses of some of the collected samples. Consequently, we can only include a description
of events; the surface rupturing history for the GF will be proposed in a future work.

5.5.1. The Rambla de los Pilares site

The Rambla de los Pilares site is in the easternmost part of sector 3 of the GF, to the east
of Castilléjar town (UTM 30S534278/4174615, Figs. 138 and 139). In this area, the fault
zone is up to 350 m wide and comprises several N30E- to N45E-striking fault strands. The
palaeoseismological analysis at the Rambla de los Pilares site is focused on a N40E-striking
S-dipping secondary strand that offsets recent fluvial deposits. In the trench area, a main gully
cuts down into lower Pleistocene sediments (ca. 2.5 Ma, Fig. 138) and is oriented SSE-NNW,
roughly perpendicular to the GF (Fig. 138).
The main geomorphological features of the study area are two flat landforms that appear on
both sides of the gully. These landforms (surfaces 1 and 2) correspond to geomorphic surfaces
dissected by the gully. Surface 1 is the main geomorphic feature in the study area. It dips gently
towards the N and is located at ca. 15–20 m above the present thalweg. This surface is the top
of a depositional fluvial terrace that varies in thickness as it is responds to the morphology of the
valley, reaching a maximum thickness of ca. 5 m. Surface 2 is a more recent fluvial terrace located
at approximately 4-5 m above the present thalweg. The fault strand in the Rambla de los Pilares
site does not present any geomorphic expression, but it juxtaposes the fluvial terrace deposits
of surface 1 against the Pleistocene deposits. The Rambla de los Pilares site includes 3 parallel
trenches (trenches 1, 2, and 3, Fig. 140) that were excavated on the western side of the gully, on
the fluvial terrace surface and across the secondary fault strand (Figs. 138 and 139).

Fig. 137. A) and B) Oblique aerial photographs showing the eroded glacis surface and the badland development
due to the incision and expansion of the drainage network in the SE (uplift) block of the Galera Fault. Location
in figure 136B.

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

5.5.2. Trench stratigraphy

We group the stratigraphic units at the Rambla de los Pilares site into bedrock units, fluvial
terrace deposits, and modern topsoil. To constrain the age of the stratigraphic units, we collected
40 charcoal samples and 9 organic-rich silt samples for radiocarbon dating. We also collected 11
samples of sand for OSL dating (Figs. 141 to 143). Radiocarbon analyses were carried out on 3
charcoal and 4 bulk samples (Figs. 141 to 143, Table 13). 5 OSL samples were sent for analysis.
The bedrock units that we observe in the trench are well-bedded sediments including silts, clays,
limestones, and gypsum veins, Lower Pleistocene in age. These deposits correspond to lacustrine
unit G4 (Fig. 94 and 138).
Fluvial terrace deposits appear unconformably over the bedrock units (Figs. 141 to 143). These
deposits are composed of two units: lower unit A, interpreted as an older fluvial terrace, and
upper unit B, interpreted as a younger fluvial terrace. In trench 1 (Fig. 141), three subunits
are distinguished within unit A: A1, A2, and A3. A1 is composed of small channel-shaped
bodies with erosive basal contacts. They consist of greyish, massive, matrix-supported,
microconglomerates with irregular gypsum and carbonate clasts and a sandy matrix. A1 overlays
the bedrock, but some conglomerate bodies are intercalated in A2. A2 unconformably overlays
A1 and the Pleistocene bedrock. It consists of greenish cemented, fine-grained sands to sandy-
silts. A3 overlays A2 and the bedrock and is made up of greyish, massive, poorly sorted, gypsum
cemented breccia with irregular, cm-sized gypsum and carbonate clasts, and sandy matrix. In
trench 2, unit A is only represented by subunit A3, while in trench 3, unit A is only represented
by subunit A2. Currently, only the age of subunit A2 is constrained with two samples from
trenches 1 and 3: Pil-1-17W (24,060 – 23,747 cal. yr BC) and Pil-3-12W (13,387 – 13,098
cal. yr BC), respectively (Figs. 141 and 143, and Table 13). Unit B lies unconformably on top
of both unit A and the bedrock units (Figs. 141 to 143). In trenches 1 and 2, unit B is a ca. 2.5
m thick wedge-shaped terrace that overlaps the underlying units (Figs. 141 and 142). In these
trenches, we distinguish two subunits within unit B: B1 and B2. Both subunits are composed of
massive brownish silts. B1 is characterised by a dark brown colour and within it we distinguish
a level of small channel-shaped bodies of microconglomerates, some levels of carbonate pebbles
and burn horizons with laterally continuous concentrations of charcoal. B2 overlies B1 and is
characterised by a light brownish colour and an erosive base. In trench 3, unit B is represented
only by a dm-thick bed of subunit B1 (Fig. 143). The age of unit B is determined by four
samples: subunit B1 is dated by samples Pil-1-6W (4,489 – 4,360 cal. yr BC) from trench 1
(Fig. 141 and Table 13), Pil-2-1W (4,453 – 4,349 cal. yr BC) from trench 2 (Fig. 142 and Table
13), and Pil-3-8W (3,715 – 3,638 cal. yr BC) from trench 3 (Fig. 143 and Table 13); subunit
B2 is dated by sample Pil-1-7W (2,339 – 2,139 cal BC) from trench 1 (Fig. 141 and Table 13).
Finally, overlying all the previously described units, we distinguish a poorly sorted, loose sandy
unit with an erosive base that includes plant roots. We interpret this unit as a modern topsoil
(Figs. 141 to 143). This top soil is the youngest unit we found at the site, with an age constrained
by sample Pil-3-7E (1,016 – 1,154 cal. yr AD) from trench C (Fig. 143 and Table 13). The ages
of these units will be more precisely constrained in the future with the OSL sample results.

5.5.3. Trench structure

In this section we describe the main structural features that are exposed on the walls of the three
trenches of the Rambla de los Pilares sites. We focus on describing faults and joints that affect
the recent fluvial terrace deposits, as well as the geometry of these deposits, i.e., tilting, folding
or shearing.

5.5.3.1. Structure of trench 1

Trench 1 is the easternmost trench in the Rambla de los Pilares site (Fig. 139). In trench 1, the
exposed fault zone consists of a ca. 8 m wide band where three ca. 60 - 70° dipping fault strands
appear (Fig. 141A): two southern S-dipping strands (F1 and F2) with fault throws higher than
the trench depth, and a northern N-dipping strand (F3) with a minor vertical throw of ca. 30
cm. F1 and F3 present simple geometries in cross-section, with damage zones constrained to
the bedrock unit. However, F2 exhibits a more complex damage zone formed by two subparallel
strands separated by ca. 0.5 m and several splays that offset both the bedrock and unit A. When
these splays reach unit A, they refract, leading to sub-vertical tension cracks (Fig. 141A and B).
We interpret these cracks as a result of coseismic refraction of extensional failure caused by a lack
of confining pressure near the surface and the existence of a tensional stress field (sensu Mercier
et al., 1983; McCalpin, 2009). The fault cores of F1, F2 and F3 feature clay gouges smeared
along the shear planes. Moreover, F1 presents a receded fault palaeo-scarp developed in the
bedrock and unit A.
The primary subhorizontal bedding of the bedrock unit is well preserved in trench 1. The bedding
gently dips to the S, and a drag fold associated with F2 is observed between F1 and F2 (Fig.
141A). Unit A shows a gentle dip towards the S over F2, as it accommodates previous erosive
relief. Unit B lies horizontally, showing an overlapping geometry over the underlying units, but
it dips towards the S, accommodating the geometry of the receded F1 scarp. Moreover, fluvial
terrace units vary in thickness along section, accommodating the previously existing relief that
was generated by the faults. Unit TS lies horizontally over the above-described units.
Several filled fissures appear in trench 1 within unit A (Fiss1 and Fiss2, Fig. 141A and B). These
fissures are related to the aforementioned fracture refraction and extensional failure associated
with the upward splaying of the F2 damage zone. These fractures are filled with unconsolidated
clast-supported microbreccias from unit A.

Fig. 141. A) Rambla de los Pilares site trench 1 logs. B) Close-up of filled fissures in the W wall. Green circles
with grey labels, non-dated radiocarbon samples, yellow circles with black labels, dated radiocarbon samples.
White circles, collected OSL samples C) Interpretation in terms of Palaeoseismic stratigraphic sequences and
events.

274 275

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

5.5.3.2. Structure of trench 2

Trench 2 is the central trench in the Rambla de los Pilares site (Fig. 139). In trench 2, although
the fault zone structure is very similar to that observed in trench 1, it is narrower (Fig. 142A). It
consists of a ca. 5 m-wide band in which we identify F2 and F3. Due to the depth of the trench,
F1 is not present, but we observe its receded scarp. As in trench 1, F2 splays and refracts upwards
offsetting unit A. In the W wall, F2 seems to affect also unit B2. In trench 2, although F3 shows
a minor throw, it offsets both the bedrock and unit A (Fig. 142A).
The entire bedrock in this trench lies horizontally, but it presents a high density of gypsum veins.
Unit A is only represented by subunit A3, which presents a fining-upwards sequence and is slightly
layered. Above F2, unit A shows a concave geometry that we interpret to be a result of fault
deformation (Fig. Fig. 142A). Units B and TS show the same geometry observed in trench 1.
In trench 2, a filled fissure (Fiss 3) is related to the F2 damage zone. As in trench 1, this fissure
is filled with unconsolidated clast-supported microbreccias from unit A.

5.5.3.3. Structure of trench 3

Trench 3 is the westernmost trench in the Rambla de los Pilares site (Fig. 139). In trench 3,
the fault zone extends along the whole trench (9 m) (Fig. 143A). The fault zone consists of a
central fault core (F0) bounded by two subvertical fault strands. The fault core consists of a
light grey, gypsum-rich, silty gouge. From the fault core, F0 branches out, leading to a damage
zone formed by several splays with centimetric throws that spread northwards and southwards:
northern splays dip to the S or are subvertical, while southern splays dip to the N. (Fig. 143A).
Most of the splays are limited to the bedrock, but some of them offset both fluvial units A and
B (Fig. 143A). In addition, some reverse splays are observed in this trench.
In trench 3, the original bedding of the bedrock unit is well preserved far from the fault core,
but it is intensely deformed close to F0 (Fig. 143A). To the S of the fault core, the bedrock unit
is fractured by fault splays, tilted northwards, and sheared against F0. To the N of the core, the
bedrock unit is highly sheared, and it only preserves remnants of the primary structure. Along
the entire fault zone, the clayish levels within the bedrock unit are smeared out along the shear
planes. In this trench, fluvial units A and B, and unit TS lie horizontally over the fault zone,
being offset by some of the fault splays and conforming to the palaeo-relief of the bedrock (Fig.
143A). Units A and B do not present internal bedding.

Interpretation in terms of Palaeoseismic stratigraphic sequences and events. In this section we present the main palaeoseismic evidence from the three trenches (1, 2, and 3)
at the Rambla de los Pilares site and its interpretation in terms of surface rupturing palaeoseismic
events. As OSL samples are still being analysed, we cannot properly constrain the ages of the
identified events a consequently still cannot propose a composite surface rupturing history for
the Rambla de los Pilares site.

5.5.4.1. Palaeoearthquake evidence in trench 1

Trench 1 contains evidence of 3 to 4 surface rupture events (events Pil1_e1 to Pil1_e4, Fig.
141C). To constrain the date of these events, we collected 11 charcoal samples and 4 organic-
rich silt samples (Fig. 141A). Radiocarbon analyses were carried out on three of these samples
(Table 13). We also collected 4 sand samples (Fig. 141A), 3 of which are being analyzed for OSL
dating.
Pil1_e1 is the oldest event recognized in trench 1 (Fig. 141C). Evidence for this event is the
rupture of fault F3, which offsets the bedrock unit and is capped by Unit A (Fig. 141). According
to this, Pil1_e1 should be older than the deposition of this fluvial terrace. Thus, this event has
a minimum age limited by sample Pil-1-17W in subunit A2 (24,060 - 23,747 cal. yr BC, Fig.
141A and Table 13), but its maximum age is limited by the date from the bedrock units (ca. 2.5
Ma). We consider the minimum age from the terrace units as a better approximation of the event
time than the maximum-limiting age from the bedrock sediments. This is because the terrace

Pil3_e1 is the oldest event represented in trench 3 (Fig. 143B). Evidence of this event is

and is capped by unit B (Fig. 143A). In other words, PilC_e1 should be younger than bedrock

\6. Regional significance of the Baza and Galera Faults.
Insights into basin evolution and tectonics

Active faults have played an important role in the recent Neogene-Quaternary evolution of
the Central Betic Cordillera. Since the late Miocene, active NNW-SSE normal faults and E-W
strike-slip faults have appeared as a consequence of ENE-WSW regional extension (Fig. 6 in
section 2) (Galindo-Zaldívar et al. 2003; Rodríguez-Fernández and Sanz de Galdeano, 2006;
Ruano et al., 2004; Alfaro et al., 2008; Haberland et al., 2017). These active faults have strongly
controlled the geometry, architecture, sedimentary environments, and landscape of the Neogene-
Quaternary basins in this sector of the cordillera.
In this section, we study the influence of the BF and the GF in the Quaternary geological history
of the Guadix-Baza Basin and the Baza Subbasin, focusing on basin capture and landscape
evolution during the subsequent exorheic stage. Moreover, from a geodynamic approach, we
integrate the results of this PhD thesis into a local tectonic model of the Baza Subbasin to
unravel the role that these faults have in the recent evolution of the Central Betic Cordillera.

6.1. The role of the Baza and Galera Faults in the evolution of
the Guadix-Baza Basin

The Guadix-Baza Basin is a tectonically active region in which several faults, folds and diapiric
structures have deformed the most recent Quaternary deposits and geomorphic features (Sanz
de Galdeano et al., 2006, 2012; Alfaro et al., 2008; García-Tortosa et al., 2008b and 2011a;
Fernández-Ibáñez et al., 2010; Castro et al., 2018). In this section, we focus on the roles that
the BF and GF have played in the capture and development of the drainage network and the
evolution of relief in the Guadix-Baza Basin.

6.1.1. Tectonic control of the Baza Fault in the Guadix-Baza Basin
capture

The role of tectonics in fluvial captures has been described by several studies (Bishop, 1995; Kong
et al., 2009; Bracciali et al., 2015; Fan et al., 2018; among many others). The tectonic control
of the Guadix-Baza Basin capture process was suggested by previous authors (e.g., Calvache and
Viseras, 1997; García-Tortosa et al., 2008a; Moral and Balanyá, 2020) However, despite the

284 285

<!-- p. 144 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

regional significance of the BF, its role in Guadix-Baza Basin capture is still unclear. We discuss
this role in this section.
All previous works addressing the capture of the Guadix-Baza Basin consider this process to have
been a single regional event (Vera, 1970; Peña, 1985; Calvache and Viseras, 1997; Azañón et
al., 2006; Díaz Hernández and Julia, 2006; Gibert et al., 2007b; among others). However, the
geomorphic analysis we carry out points out to an evolutionary model of the capture involving
two main phases (double capture).
The capture zone of the Guadix-Baza Basin was located in the western sector (Guadix Subbasin)
(Calvache and Viseras, 1997; García-Tortosa et al., 2008a and 2011a; Moral and Balanyá, 2020)
in the footwall of the BF (Figs. 13 and 144). Although our calculations indicate that this fault
presents a low uplift rate (0.1-0.5 mm/yr), the relative offset of the fault blocks is responsible for
the topographic difference between the western (footwall) and eastern (hanging wall) sectors of
the Guadix-Baza Basin. Therefore, when capture was initiated, the relief of the basin was higher
in the western sector (Guadix Subbasin, BF upthrown block) than in the eastern sector (Baza
Subbasin, BF downthrown block). This tectonic process was also responsible for the general
sedimentary architecture of the basin during the Plio-Pleistocene endorheic stage of the basin
(Alfaro et al., 2008). This sedimentary architecture was charaterised by a fluvial system in the BF
upthrown block (the PalaeoFardes River) that drained eastwards to the BF downthrown block
(Baza Palaeolake) (Fig. 11 and stage 0 in Fig. 144). Taking into account this palaeotopography
and the continuous activity of the BF (Alfaro et al., 2008 and 2021), the Baza Palaeolake
remained active during a poorly constrained period after the fluvial capture of the western sector.
Thus, the activity of the BF during the Quaternary was responsible for a double capture in
the Guadix-Baza Basin: an initial fluvial capture of the western sector and a second lacustrine
capture of the eastern sector.
We summarize the evolution of the double capture of the Guadix-Baza Basin in figure 144, in
which the main events are indicated.
At the end of the endorheic stage, the headward erosion of a tributary of the Guadalquivir River
reached the divide between the Guadix-Baza Basin and the Guadalquivir Basin (stage 1a in Fig.
144 and Fig. 145). At this moment, erosion started in the western sector of the Guadix-Baza
Basin, but it was initially constrained to a small area around the capture zone. The first phase
of the capture process was initiated when the abovementioned tributary of the Guadalquivir
River reached the first river of the Guadix-Baza Basin fluvial network (stage 1b in Fig. 144). At
this moment, the tributary turned into the Guadiana Menor River. As the headward erosion
of the Guadiana Menor River proceeded, it eventually reached the main river of the western
sector of the Guadix-Baza Basin, i.e., the PalaeoFardes River (stage 1c in Fig. 144 and Fig 145).
This moment was a milestone in the evolution of the basin, as it implied the capture of most
of the drainage network in the western sector. Prior to this event, most of the rivers in this
western sector previously drained eastward to the Baza Palaeolake (e.g., Palaeo-Fardes, Baúl, Gor,
Guadahortuna, and Guadalentín Rivers); however, from this point forward, they drained to the
Guadalquivir Basin. Moreover, as the BF represented a topographic barrier, the Baza Palaeolake
remained uncaptured. After the capture of the PalaeoFardes River, headward erosion progressed
in all directions along the western sector (stage 1d in Fig. 144). During this phase most of the
western sector was already exorheic, but the eastern sector remained endorheic. Consequently, Fig. 144. Evolutionary model of the Middle Pleistocene double capture process of the Guadix-Baza Basin. This
from the start of capture until this moment, fluvial incision progressed in the western sector but process involved two phases: the capture of the fluvial system of the western sector (stages 1a to 1d) and the
did not affect the eastern sector. capture of the Baza Palaeolake in the eastern sector (stages 2a to 2c).

of the GF, the model is similar. As most of the GF displacement consists of horizontal motion
and presents a very low uplift rate, this structure did not produce significant accommodation
space on the downthrown block (NW). Therefore, the sedimentation rates should have been
considerably higher than the vertical displacement of the fault, leading to the complete burial
of the fault plane and masking a possible mountain front (Fig. 146A). This is supported by the
presence of lacustrine deposits that crop out extensively in both fault blocks (Fig. 94).
Furthermore, during the endorheic stage of the basin, both active faults were covered by poorly
lithified, water-saturated sediments. As a result, they developed unique, highly complex and
heterogeneous inner fault zone structures, where fault rocks present brittle-ductile deformation
(section 4.4.). Recent palaeoseismological trenches along the GF have exposed similar
extraordinary fault rocks below recent fluvial terraces, and consequently, a similar 3D trench
study is planned in the future (Trench C in Fig. 140).
The end of the endorheic stage of the Guadix-Baza Basin, i.e., basin capture, can be considered
a key event for the geological and geomorphological evolution of the region. At this point,

The mountain front of the BF (Fig. 40) is described by García-Tortosa et al. (2008a). They
define this as an up to 100 m-high mountain front that indicates high activity according to the

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

geomorphic indices that they applied. In addition, they suggest that the presence of this mountain
front, despite the high erodibility of the Pliocene to Pleistocene sediments that crop out in both
the footwall and hanging wall, is further evidence of fault activity (García-Tortosa et al., 2008a).
The higher fluvial incision in the BF upthrown block is responsible for the development of the
spectacular badland landscapes in the Guadix Subbasin (Figs. 14A and 146C).
In the case of the GF, it does not present a clearly observable mountain front. The main reason is
that the GF is a strike-slip fault with a minor vertical component. Hence, the GF is characterised
by a very low uplift rate. In addition, the highly erodible lacustrine sedimentary rocks cropping
out in both fault blocks (mostly silts, gypsum, sands, and clays) have been intensely eroded
since the basin became exorheic. Therefore, the fault-related uplift was rapidly erased by the
development of a large number of catchments, leading to the intricate drainage system of the
present study area. According to Bull and McFadden (1977), when the present erosional front
is more than 1 km from the initial front, the fault can be considered inactive. However, this is
applied to mountain range-front faults, where the uplifted block is usually composed of hard
(basement) rocks. The case of the GF is different, as it presents, in both blocks, highly erodible
Pliocene to Pleistocene sedimentary rocks (Fig. 94). Consequently, although the fault is active,
erosional processes have had a marked impact on the uplifted block, resulting in a highly receded
erosional front (Figs. 136 and 146C). The Guadix-Baza Basin is an exceptional example that can
be used as a model to understand the relationship between the landscape evolution and active
faults in other captured basins.

Fig. 146 (see next page). Schematic sketches and sections illustrating the evolution of the geomorphic
expression of the Baza and Galera faults and their impact on the landscape. A) During the endorheic stage
of the basin, the faults did not develop a significant geomorphological expression as they were covered by
sediments. B) After the basin capture the BF and GF began to have an imprint on the landscape, giving rise to
topographic scarpments along their traces. C) At present day, the fault uplift and the intense fluvial incision has
favoured badland landscapes in the fault upthrowns.

6.1.3. Impact of active faults on the recent drainage system of the We interpret these steeper profiles as a result of the slope gradient produced by the increasing
Guadix-Baza Basin vertical displacement of the BF from the fault tips towards its central part (Alfaro et al. 2008;
Medina Cascales et al. 2020). In summary, the Baza and Castril rivers are interpreted as lateral
rivers of the BF.There are several examples of the impact of active faults on the development of drainage networks
(e.g., Gürbüz et al., 2015; Zielke et al., 2015; Gallen and Wegmann, 2017; Authemayou et
al., 2018). This subject has been studied in strike-slip geodynamic settings within the Betic
Cordillera (Wenzens and Wenzens, 1997; Mather, 2000; Silva et al., 1993 and 2003; Booth-Rea
et al., 2004; Whitfield and Harvey, 2012; Giaconia et al., 2013), but not in the ENE-WSW
extensional conditions that characterise the study area.
Since basin capture, the differential uplift between fault blocks has generated a geomorphic
impact on the rivers in the new catchment area generated after the endorheic-exorheic transition,
the Guadiana Menor catchment (Fig. 3). This impact is evidenced by differences in valley
incision between the western and eastern sectors of the Guadix-Baza Basin (Fig. 147). PérezPeña et al. (2009) calculated the volume of sediment loss since the late Pliocene and erosion
rates for the Guadix-Baza Basin, obtaining much higher values for the Guadix Subbasin, i.e., in
the BF upthrown block. In our study, we constructed a map that represents the incision of the
fluvial network in the entire Guadix-Baza Basin by displaying the height difference between the
reconstructed glacis and the present topographic surface. The results of this analysis (Fig. 147)
show different incision patterns in the eastern and western sectors of the basin. Considering
analogous distances from the capture point of the basin, incision is higher in the western Guadix
Sector (upthrown block of the BF) than in the eastern Baza sector (downthrown block of the
BF). Our results reflect that despite the low uplift rate, BF activity strongly controls valley
incision in the Guadix-Baza Basin; in other words, the asymmetry in valley incision is the result
of the landscape response to the relative uplift of the two fault blocks. In the case of the western
Guadix Subbasin, higher tectonic uplift favours vertical fluvial incision and the development
of narrow, deep valleys with sharp ridges. These features represent some of the most iconic
landscapes of the Granada Geopark, such as the Gorafe and Guadix badlands (Fig. 14A). In
contrast, due to the lower relative uplift of the eastern Baza Subbasin, vertical valley incision is,
in general, lower. Consequently, in this sector, extensive erosion dominates, which has led to the
development of wider and fewer downcut valleys (Fig. 147).

6.2. Significance of the Baza Subbasin in the geodynamic
setting of the Betic Cordillera

In this section, we propose a local tectonic model for the Baza Subbasin involving both the Baza
and Galera Faults. For this purpose, we discuss the implications of the results obtained from
the Baza GPS network for the tectonic setting and seismic hazard assessment of the study area.
In addition, this tectonic model is integrated in the regional geodynamic setting of the Central
Betic Cordillera.

6.2.1. Displacement transfer between the Baza and Galera Faults

The Baza and Galera Faults constitute an active fault system in the Baza Subbasin. The interaction
of synchronously active faults implies a transfer of displacement between them (e.g., Dahlstrom,
1969; Walsh and Watterson, 1991). Moreover, in active fault systems where different types of
faults coexist, kinematic coherence is expected; that is, the displacement rates of the interacting
faults should be interdependent. Analyses of kinematic coherence within fault systems are usually
approached by applying field data to elucidate fault attitudes, slip directions and displacement
rates (Mouslopoulou et al., 2007 and 2008; Nicol et al., 2010). In the Baza Subbasin, our GPS
data provide slip rates that permit us to analyse how displacement is transferred between the
normal BF and the strike-slip GF to elucidate whether the fault system is kinematically coherent.
As mentioned in sections 4.3.2.2. and 5.3.2.2., the residual velocity vectors that we obtain can
be grouped into two sets (Figs. 62 and 123): i) set 1 comprising sites 5200, 5500, and 5700
with higher residual velocities (E component between -1.2 ± 0.5 and -1.4 ± 0.4 mm/yr) and ii)
set 2 including sites 5100, 5400, and 5600 with lower residual velocities (E component ranging
between -0.7 ± 0.6 and -0.8 ± 0.5 mm/yr). Set 1 includes all the sites located in the footwall of
the BF and in the NW block of the GF (Fig. 150). Set 2 includes the rest of the sites, i.e., all the
sites located in the hanging wall of the BF and in the SE block of the GF (Fig. 150). According
to these observations and using caution because of the low signal-to-noise ratio observed at
the 95% confidence level, we propose that the Baza Subbasin could be divided into two main
tectonic blocks (Fig. 150B): i) a southeastern block made up of the hanging wall of the BF and
the SE block of the GF; and ii) a northwestern block made up of the footwall of the BF and Fig. 150. A) Sketch representing the geodynamic setting of the Central and Eastern Betic Cordillera. Black
the NW block of the GF. To test the displacement transfer and kinematic coherence of the Baza traces are the main Quaternary-active faults. The green area represents the extension-dominated Central Betic
Subbasin fault system, we use our GPS data to analyse the slip azimuths and displacement rates Cordillera. Green arrows indicate regional extension. The yellow area is the strike-slip-dominated Eastern Betic
Cordillera. Yellow arrows indicate strike-slip motion along the main active faults. The red dashed line remarksof the Baza and Galera Faults. A requirement in synchronously active fault systems involving
the position of the Baza Subbasin. B) Tectonic sketch of the Baza Subbasin showing the kinematic couplingonly two tectonic blocks is that the slip directions of all faults within the system must be sub- between the Baza and Galera Faults. GPS sites are indicated.
parallel (Mouslopoulou et al., 2007). In the Baza Subbasin fault system, this slip parallelism is
confirmed by our residual velocity vectors, which present a sub-parallel WSW azimuth with
respect to stable Eurasia (Fig. 61). Global data compiled by Mouslopoulou et al. (2007) indicate
that in kinematically coherent fault systems involving normal and strike-slip faults, extension
and strike-slip rates are equal (Fig.151). Our GPS-derived vectors seem to indicate that this
is also the case in the Baza Subbasin fault system, as the extension rates on the normal BF are
within the range of the strike-slip rates on the GF (VN065E = 0.7 ± 0.4 mm/yr and VN065E =
0.5 ± 0.3 mm/yr, respectively; Fig. 151 and Tables 3 and 11).

0.9 ± 0.4 and 1.3 ± 0.4 mm/yr. This implies that the BF accommodates approximately one-third
of the total regional extension, underlining the seismogenic potential of this active structure.
Moreover, recent CGPS data from the Topo-Iberia network in the Central Betic Cordillera
(Galindo-Zaldívar et al., 2015) indicate that the regional extension in the area located east of the
Sierra Nevada is approximately 0.8 ± 0.4 mm/yr (Fig. 7). Consequently, the BF accommodates
almost all the extension in this area. The results obtained from the GPS-derived data indicate
that the BF accommodates one-third of the regional extension of the Central Betic Cordillera
and that this structure represents the easternmost active normal fault in the region.
The residual velocities that we obtain seem to indicate that the BF and GF are kinematically
coherent and that the Baza Subbasin is divided into two main tectonic blocks with no significant
internal deformation. The southeastern block comprises the hanging wall of the BF and the SE
block of the GF, while the northwestern block comprises the footwall of the BF and the NW
block of the GF (Fig. 150B). This interpretation also implies that the Baza and Galera Faults are
kinematically coupled and physically linked and that a potential complex rupture involving both
faults should be considered in seismic hazard assessment studies.

6.2.3. Implications for seismic hazard assessment

The geodetic slip rates on the Baza and Galera Faults, estimated for the first time by Alfaro et
al. (2021), will facilitate future seismic hazard assessment studies in this region. The proposed
tectonic model (see section 12.1) has implications for the seismic potential calculation of this
area. As we have already mentioned, the active extension in the Central Betic Cordillera is mainly

\7. Conclusions

The Baza Subbasin is located in the Central Betic Cordillera, which is dominated by extensional
tectonics. The main active structures in the subbasin are the Baza Fault (BF) and the Galera Fault
(GF).
The BF is the easternmost active normal fault in the Central Betic Cordillera, accommodating
one-third of the WSW-ENE regional extension. In addition, the BF is the main active structure
in the Guadix-Baza Basin and is kinematically connected to the strike-slip GF. Consequently,
these faults divide the Baza Subbasin into two main tectonic blocks with no significant internal
deformation: a southeastern block comprising the BF footwall and the GF SE block and a
northwestern block that comprises the BF footwall and the GF NW block. Both tectonic blocks
exhibit westward displacement, but the relative velocities between them result in extension along
the BF that is transferred to the E along the left-lateral GF.
The study we carry out in the Baza Subbasin leads us to properly characterise these two active
faults by integrating different disciplines, such as structural geology, geomorphology, seismology,
and geodesy. We summarize below our results and interpretations.

About fault geometry

In the study area, deposits belonging to the sedimentary infill of the Baza Subbasin crop out.
Therefore, the faults we observe correspond to the surface expression of the upward propagation
of basement faults through the sedimentary overburden.
The BF is an approximately 40 km-long, E-dipping normal fault that offsets upper Miocene to
Holocene fluvio-alluvial and lacustrine deposits. At the map-scale, the surface fault array can be
divided into two sectors according to its geometry: i) a northern sector striking N-S to NNW-
SSE (N175E), arranged in a narrow damage zone (a few metres to ca. 1 km); and ii) a southern
sector striking NW-SE (N165E to N135E) characterised by a broad damage zone that widens
southwards (from 1 to 7 km). These orientations reflect the subsurface geometry of the BF. The
along-strike geometric variations along the two fault sectors are caused by: i) a different basement
in the north and south of the Baza Subbasin that influences the fault geometry at depth and thus
the geometry of the damage zone in the sedimentary cover; ii) the different orientations of the
basement BF with respect to the WSW-ENE regional extension; and iii) the interaction between
the damage zones of the BF and other active faults near the fault terminations.

Sería recomendable caracterizar otras fallas secundarias de la subcuenca para precisar este
modelo. En especial, sería interesante analizar la estructura de Alfahuara-Botardo, situada

310 311

<!-- p. 157 -->

Geometría, cinemática y paleosismicidad de las zonas de falla de Baza y Galera Iván Medina Cascales

en el NE de la zona de estudio, y el sistema de fallas del corredor del Almanzora, en la
parte meridional. Además, sería muy útil estudiar las zonas de interacción entre estas
fallas activas y las de Baza y Galera, así como analizar su posible conexión sísmica. Para
cuantificar esta investigación sería necesario mejorar y precisar la red GPS de la subcuenca
de Baza, con la construcción de nuevos puntos de control.
• Una de las principales características de la Cuenca de Guadix-Baza y, en particular, de la
subcuenca de Baza, es su excepcional registro paleontológico. Éste incluye yacimientos de
macrovertebrados que se han preservado en la sucesión estratigráfica fluvio-lacustre Plio-
Cuaternaria. En varios casos, existe un intenso debate sobre la edad de estos yacimientos,
y son pocas las investigaciones que han tenido en cuenta su contexto tectónico de detalle.
La realización de varios estudios geológicos de detalle, que tengan en cuenta las estructuras
activas (pliegues y fallas) sería de gran utilidad para resolver algunos de estos debates sobre
la edad de los yacimientos.
• Finalmente, con relación a los aspectos geológicos y geomorfológicos de la Cuenca de
Guadix-Baza, sería muy conveniente precisar la edad de los depósitos endorreicos más
recientes y de los sedimentos exorreicos más antiguos. Esto permitiría conocer con
mayor precisión cuándo se produjo la captura de la cuenca. Esta edad es clave para
diferentes estudios, como el cálculo de las tasas de erosión o de las tasas de deformación
a partir del glacis.
